I. INTRODUCTION 
Defendants' five motions to dismiss share a common thesis: that the OCHCA 
Remedial Action Statement ("RAS") closure of the Huntington Beach Navigation 
Center ("HBNC") immunizes them from RCRA citizen suit liability regardless of 
what the ground actually contained. That thesis fails on the law, the facts, and three 
categories of newly discovered evidence attached hereto as Exhibits A-1 through J-1 
and incorporated by reference. 
Defendants' motions fail for three core reasons. 
 
First, Plaintiff has Article III standing. Plaintiff suffers an ongoing, legally compelled 
injury—at least sixty separate visits to a pharmacy 549 feet from the HBNC to fill 
Schedule II, IV, and V controlled substances that cannot be delivered by mail. Each 
visit places Plaintiff within the Site's documented contaminant migration zone, 
exposed to ambient air and dust potentially contaminated with hexavalent chromium 
(Cr(VI)), lead, and arsenic. This is not a generalized grievance; it is a documented, 
repeated, and involuntary exposure to toxic substances. 
 
Second, the OCHCA NFA is not entitled to deference and is void ab initio. It was 
issued under the wrong APNs, before Cr(VI) results were in hand, without public 
notice, by an agency statutorily barred from overseeing City-owned sites, based on a 
fabricated "open air tent encampment" site description, and with two express DTSC 
Case 8:26-cv-00348-JWH-ADS     Document 130     Filed 09/09/26     Page 2 of 373   Page

conditions unsatisfied. An agency action that is a legal nullity— issued without 
jurisdiction, based on false facts, and in violation of statutory conditions— is entitled 
to zero deference. Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo, 144 S. Ct. 2244 (2024), and 
even the residual Skidmore standard do not save it.  
 
Third, the SAVC plausibly alleges that each defendant contributed to an ongoing, 
imminent, and substantial endangerment. The defendants' own conduct at the 
Huntington Beach Civic Center—where they applied every required remedial 
measure to a PAH site six miles away—establishes the standard of care they 
knowingly failed to apply at the HBNC. The HHAP MOU confirms the 
endangerment is ongoing: the Sprung Structure base seal has failed, the water supply 
is contaminated, and the contamination was never remediated— it was merely 
deferred to a redevelopment timeline that expired in August 2025 with zero 
development initiated.  
Because Rule 12(b)(6) requires the Court to accept Plaintiff’s factual allegations as 
true, defendants’ attempts to dispute contamination levels, exposure pathways, or 
regulatory chronology are improper and cannot support dismissal. The motions to 
dismiss should be denied in their entirety.

II. PROCEDURAL HISTORY 
On July 13, 2026, this Court denied defendants' motions to dismiss the First 
Amended Complaint and granted Plaintiff leave to amend (ECF 105). Plaintiff filed 
the Second Amended Verified Complaint ("SAVC") on August 17, 2026 (ECF 115), 
adding new evidentiary allegations based on evidence obtained through CPRA 
requests and subsequent investigations. Defendants now re-file their motions to 
dismiss, re-litigating arguments this Court has already rejected. 
The SAVC amplifies—and does not retreat from— the FAVC. The factual allegations 
in the SAVC are more detailed and supported by new evidence, but they arise from 
the same nucleus of operative facts. If the FAVC was sufficient, the SAVC is 
necessarily sufficient. Eminence Capital, LLC v. Aspeon, Inc., 316 F.3d 1048, 1052 
(9th Cir. 2003). 
 
III. LEGAL STANDARD 
A complaint survives Rule 12(b)(6) if it alleges "enough facts to state a claim to 
relief that is plausible on its face." Bell Atl. Corp. v. Twombly, 550 U.S. 544, 570 
(2007). Courts accept all factual allegations as true and construe them in the plaintiff's 
favor. Ashcroft v. Iqbal, 556 U.S. 662, 678 (2009). Pro se pleadings are "held to less 
stringent standards than formal pleadings drafted by lawyers." Haines v. Kerner, 404 
U.S. 519, 520 (1972).

Under RCRA § 7002(a)(1)(B), 42 U.S.C. § 6972(a)(1)(B), any person may sue any 
person "who has contributed or who is contributing to the past or present handling, 
storage, treatment, transportation, or disposal of any solid or hazardous waste which 
may present an imminent and substantial endangerment to health or the 
environment." The standard is permissive—may  present, not "does present" or "has 
presented." Meghrig v. KFC Western, Inc., 516 U.S. 479, 485-86 (1996). 
 
IV. PLAINTIFF HAS STANDING 
A. Plaintiff's Ongoing Exposure Is Documented and Legally Compelled 
Plaintiff regularly fills prescriptions at the Walgreens Pharmacy located at 17522 
Beach Boulevard, Huntington Beach, CA 92647. The straight-line distance from this 
pharmacy to the Huntington Beach Navigation Center (17631 Cameron Lane / 17642 
Beach Boulevard) is 549 feet. (Exhibit J-1). Plaintiff's prescription history (Exhibit J-
1) documents at least sixty (60) separate fill dates at this location between September 
2, 2024 and August 31, 2026, occurring approximately every two to four weeks. 
Among Plaintiff's prescriptions are Schedule II and IV controlled substances that 
legally require in-person pickup and cannot be delivered by mail or automatically 
refilled under California Health and Safety Code § 11159 and applicable DEA 
regulations. These include D-Amphetamine ER 20mg and 30mg Salt Combo 
(Schedule II), Oxycodone/Acetaminophen 10-325mg (Schedule II), Tramadol 50mg

(Schedule IV), Clonazepam 0.5mg (Schedule IV), and Pregabalin 150mg (Schedule 
V). Plaintiff's pharmacy visits are not discretionary— they are legally mandated. 
During each visit, Plaintiff was physically present within 549 feet of the HBNC and 
within the Site's documented contaminant migration zone. The migration zone 
includes preferential transport pathways through abandoned wells W-17305 and W-
5873 and the unconnected Cameron Lane sewer rough-in, as documented in the 
SAVC. Each visit exposed Plaintiff to ambient air and dust potentially contaminated 
with hexavalent chromium (Cr(VI)), lead, arsenic, and other toxic substances 
originating from the Site. 
This is not a single past exposure but an ongoing series of legally compelled contacts 
with contaminated air and dust. Each visit is a separate, documented exposure event. 
Adkins v. VIM Recycling, Inc., 644 F.3d 483, 494 (7th Cir. 2011) found that ongoing 
exposure to contaminated property confers standing where plaintiff can show 
continued presence. The 549-foot distance is not speculative; it is a measured fact 
supported by the SAVC's documentation of preferential transport pathways through 
abandoned wells and the unconnected sewer rough-in. Plaintiff's documented, 
ongoing, and legally compelled presence within the Site's contaminant migration 
zone establishes both Article III standing and the "imminent and substantial 
endangerment" required by RCRA § 6972(a)(1)(B). Friends of the Earth, Inc. v. 
Laidlaw Envtl. Servs., 528 U.S. 167, 180-81 (2000).

B. Liberty Park Water Association Well 01 Cr(VI) Detection 
After the filing of the SAVC, Plaintiff obtained evidence that hexavalent chromium 
has been detected in Well 01 of the Liberty Park Water Association at concentrations 
of 0.41 ȝg/L, 0.42 ȝg/L, and 0.43 ȝg/L (2024-2026)—21 times California's Public 
Health Goal of 0.02 ȝg/L (Exhibit I-1). The City of Huntington Beach holds a 
proprietary ownership interest in the Liberty Park Water Association, as documented 
in Grant Deed CC-1142, dated 12/18/2008, APN 165-311-24. 
This detection is significant on three independent grounds: 
 
First, it establishes a contamination pathway extending beyond the South Midway 
City Mutual Water Company North Well previously documented in the SAVC (0.95 
ȝg/L, May 21, 2026, 47.5× California's Public Health Goal), demonstrating that the 
contamination footprint is broader than previously alleged. 
 
Second, the City's proprietary interest in the affected water association renders the 
City a responsible party whose own infrastructure has been contaminated by the 
endangerment it created and concealed. 
 
Third, the geographic relationship between Well 01 and the HBNC site is consistent 
with the downgradient migration pattern established by existing sampling data, 
independently corroborating the pathway theory already pled in the SAVC. The

pathway for human exposure. 
Cr(VI) detections in public water supply are independently sufficient to establish 
imminent endangerment, even without migration pathway proof. 
 
C. Causation and Redressability 
Each defendant contributed to the conditions causing Plaintiff's injury. 
City of Huntington Beach: But for the City's decision to acquire the HBNC through 
the Housing Authority before completing environmental review (Resolution No. 06, 
April 6, 2020) and its subsequent certification of approximately $2.26 million in 
CARES Act expenditures through City Manager Oliver Chi with contemporaneous 
knowledge of hazardous materials, residents would not have been housed on a toxic 
site. RCRA does not require proximate causation, only contribution. 
 
OCHCA/County of Orange: But for OCHCA's fabrication of the "open air tent 
encampment" characterization— which eliminated vapor intrusion pathway analysis 
and bypassed CEQA State Clearinghouse routing—and its issuance of an NFA 
without jurisdiction under Cal. H&SC § 25262(a)(4), a full remediation would have 
been required. RCRA does not require proximate causation, only contribution.

EEC Environmental: But for EEC's retroactive annotation of boring logs and its

RCRA does not require proximate causation, only contribution. 
 
RWQCB/Lindberg: But for Lindberg's failure to exercise his independent authority 
under Cal. Water Code § 13304 despite Plaintiff's documented August 2025 
complaint, the contamination would have been addressed. RCRA does not require 
proximate causation, only contribution. 
 
Mercy House: But for Mercy House's operation of a residential shelter on confirmed 
contaminated ground while its CEO falsely represented to HUD in May 2026 that 
Cr(VI) "was not detected in groundwater"—in the same month the SCMCWC North 
Well recorded 0.95 ȝg/L— residents would not have been exposed. RCRA does not 
require proximate causation, only contribution. 
 
A favorable RCRA judgment requiring defendants to conduct a HHRA, prepare and 
implement a RAW, seal abandoned well W-4150, and record a Covenant to Restrict 
Use would redress Plaintiff's injuries. Defendants applied every one of these 
measures six miles away at the Civic Center.

V. DEFENDANTS' OWN CONDUCT AT THE CIVIC CENTER 
ESTABLISHES THE STANDARD OF CARE THEY VIOLATED 
Fourteen documents from DTSC Site Code 401457-11 (Exhibits C-1, D-1, E-1) 
establish what the City of Huntington Beach, under DTSC supervision, required for 
comparable or lesser contamination at a site with no residential occupants: 
x DTSC project team assignment within weeks of discovery; 
x A Human Health Risk Assessment ("HHRA") within four months (October 27, 
2008); 
x A DTSC-approved Removal Action Workplan ("RAW") within five months 
(December 18, 2008); 
x Physical remediation of 247 cubic yards with vapor barriers and 4-inch 
concrete caps, with all workers HAZWOPER-trained; 
x Post-remediation air and soil confirmation sampling; and 
x A Covenant to Restrict Use recorded April 24, 2012 permanently prohibiting 
residential use, hospitals, schools for persons under 21, and day care on the 
contaminated footprint. 
DTSC continues to charge the City $42,919 annually for ongoing oversight of that 
site (Exhibit E-1).

The contaminants at the Civic Center—PAHs—are classified Group 2A/2B by 
IARC. Cr(VI) is classified Group 1—the highest carcinogenicity classification. 
The Covenant's permanent prohibition maps exactly onto the HBNC's actual use: 174 
residential occupants living directly above confirmed Cr(VI) contamination at 3.27× 
residential RSL. 
EEC Environmental authored the 2023 Soil Management Plan and 2024 Five-Year 
Review for the Civic Center (Exhibits D-1, E-1), requiring DTSC-compliant vapor 
intrusion analysis, advance notice of field activities, and annual monitoring. At the 
same time, EEC's project manager, Laura Holder, retroactively annotated the 
HBNC Phase II boring map three days before those borings were physically 
drilled. (PDF metadata: username "lholder"; SAVC ¶ 57). EEC cannot claim 
ignorance of applicable regulatory standards; it was applying those standards at the 
Civic Center under DTSC supervision while allegedly subverting them at the HBNC. 
Defendants applied every one of the required remedial measures—HHRA, RAW, 
vapor barrier, concrete cap, HAZWOPER training, post-remediation sampling, and a 
permanent Covenant to Restrict Use—six miles away . They are known, available, 
and capable of being ordered by this Court. Their failure to apply them at the HBNC 
was not an oversight. It was a decision.

VI. THE OCHCA NFA IS NOT ENTITLED TO DEFERENCE AND  
IS VOID AB INITIO 
Defendants urge deference to the OCHCA NFA. This argument fails for four 
independent reasons. 
A. Loper Bright Abrogated Chevron Deference 
Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo, 144 S. Ct. 2244 (2024), abrogated Chevron 
deference. Courts now independently interpret statutes without deferring to agency 
interpretations. Even under the residual Skidmore standard, Skidmore v. Swift & Co., 
323 U.S. 134, 140 (1944), deference requires "power to persuade." An NFA issued 
on fabricated site conditions, under wrong parcel numbers, before Cr(VI) results were 
in hand, with two express DTSC conditions unsatisfied, by an agency statutorily 
barred from overseeing the site under Cal. H&SC § 25262(a)(4), has no persuasive 
power—and certainly not enough to overcome Plaintiff's factual allegations at the 
pleading stage. 
 
B. DTSC Itself Has Repudiated the Sufficiency of the Instrument 
DTSC's October 8, 2024 letter on the Samuel L. Jones Hall Homeless Shelter 
Improvements project (Exhibit A-1)—a project involving only exterior improvements 
to an already-operating shelter (parking, pathways, modular shower/restroom)—
establishes DTSC's formal institutional position:

"DTSC does not recommend that a SGMP, or a Soil Management Plan (SMP), be 
used as a primary cleanup plan to address contamination within the soil present at 
the site. A SMP or SGMP alone cannot sufficiently identify and document the 
potential contaminants that may pose a threat to human health and the environment. 
DTSC recommends that a cleanup plan, a Removal Action Workplan (RAW) or 
Remedial Action Plan (RAP), be prepared to adequately address all site impacts after 
complete characterization." [Exhibit A-1; Comment 1] 
 
"DTSC recommends the City of Santa Rosa enter into a voluntary agreement to 
address contamination at brownfields and other types of properties or receive 
oversight from a self-certified local agency, DTSC or Regional Water Quality 
Control Board." [Exhibit A-1; Comment 2] 
 
The Sam Jones Hall letter (Exhibit A-1)— authored by DTSC's CEQA Unit through 
the very process the EO bypassed—shows exactly what DTSC would have said if 
either key had been removed. DTSC’s CEQA Unit is the exact entity whose review 
was bypassed at the HBNC, making its Sam Jones Hall guidance uniquely probative. 
If a Soil Management Plan "alone cannot sufficiently identify and document the 
potential contaminants that may pose a threat to human health and the environment" 
for a shelter parking lot expansion, then the OCHCA RAS form— a document weaker

The Sam Jones Hall project (Exhibit A-1) received DTSC scrutiny because it 
proceeded through normal CEQA review—filing an IS/MND, receiving a State 
Clearinghouse number, routing through inter-agency comment. The HBNC bypassed 
this process. Escobedo's April 24, 2020 email cited Executive Order N-32-20 to 
suspend H&SC §§ 50214 and 50219 and eliminate CEQA review— simultaneously 
preventing State Clearinghouse routing, eliminating IS/MND preparation, and 
foreclosing DTSC's CEQA Unit from issuing the very comments it issued in Santa 
Rosa. 
 
C. The NFA Is Void Ab Initio on Nine Independent Grounds 
The NFA is void on any single ground; the convergence of all nine is dispositive. 
These grounds fall into three categories: jurisdictional defects, procedural defects, 
and factual falsity. 
 
Jurisdictional Defects: 
1. Statutory Bar: Cal. H&SC § 25262(a)(4) prohibits OCHCA oversight of City-
owned sites. OCHCA Case No. 20IC002 was issued in violation of this 
provision— a ground OCHCA does not address in its MTD.

2. Wrong APNs: The NFA was issued under APNs 167-042-08 and 167-472-07 
rather than the correct APNs 167-472-08/09, confirmed by quitclaim deeds. 
The NFA does not cover the correct Beach Boulevard parcel (APN 167-472-
09, not 167-472-07). 
 
Procedural Defects: 
3. Unsatisfied DTSC Conditions: DTSC Engineer Senga's April 27, 2020 
conditions— deeper sampling and soil gas collection— were never satisfied. As 
Senga wrote: "However I would suggest you sample beyond 3 ft bgs and also 
collect soil gas data." 
4. No Public Notice: No public notice was issued under Cal. H&SC § 101480. 
5. Authorization Before Data: Authorization issued before Cr(VI) results were 
in hand. 
6. GeoTracker Records: GeoTracker records were retroactively assembled in 
March 2025. 
 
Factual Falsity: 
7. Fabricated Site Description: The "open air tent encampment" 
characterization eliminated vapor intrusion pathway analysis and was the sole 
origin of the CEQA bypass. 
8. No Valid RAS: No valid RAS exists for Beach Boulevard.

D. Stanphill: Division Chief-Level Deliberate Non-Engagement 
When Plaintiff contacted DTSC regarding the HBNC, DTSC— through Benjamin 
Stanphill— stated that it "wouldn't say anything different" about the OCHCA NFA. A 
conference profile (Exhibit B-1) confirms that Stanphill is the Southern California 
Division Chief for DTSC's Site Mitigation and Restoration Program. He oversees 
approximately 100 staff, primarily project managers for southern California sites in 
voluntary cleanups, RCRA Corrective Action, Schools, and Discovery and 
Enforcement groups. He is a licensed Civil Engineer. His listed expertise includes 
vapor intrusion—precisely the exposure pathway that the " open air" fabrication was 
designed to eliminate. His contact number carries a 714-area code— Orange 
County—placing him at the DTSC Cypress office with direct jurisdictional authority 
over the HBNC. 
Stanphill personally appeared on the CC list of the October 24, 2023 DTSC SMP 
Approval Letter for the Civic Center (Exhibit D-1), in his then-title of Senior 
Hazardous Substances Engineer. He was actively reviewing vapor barrier 
requirements for a PAH site at City Hall while simultaneously, as Division Chief, 
declining to engage with a Cr(VI) site in his own jurisdiction—a site involving an

VII. THE HHAP MOU CONFIRMS ONGOING ENDANGERMENT 
The April 29, 2026 HHAP MOU (Exhibit III; incorporated by reference) is a party-
opponent admission of ongoing endangerment on three independent grounds: 
 
First, engineering control failure. The MOU acknowledges that the Sprung 
Structure base seal— the only barrier between residents and contaminated soil—has 
failed and commits $75,000 for "necessary improvements." This confirms the 
exposure pathway was never eliminated and that the "cap" required by the Covenant 
was never intact.

Second, planned ground disturbance. The repairs necessarily involve ground

repaving of asphalt, structural improvements, and potential import of fill material—
all of which DTSC said triggers full RAW/RAP and oversight requirements in the 
Sam Jones Hall letter. 
 
Third, prospective RCRA violation. The City and Mercy House plan to perform 
this work with residents present, with no HAZWOPER-trained workers, no vapor 
intrusion assessment, and no DTSC oversight. This is a future violation that is 
"imminent and substantial" under RCRA's permissive standard. See Meghrig, 516 
U.S. at 485-86 ("may present" standard). 
 
RCRA's endangerment provision is designed to reach "continued pollution arising 
from past RCRA violations." Meghrig, 516 U.S. at 485. The MOU proves the 
contamination was never remedied— it was merely deferred. The LMIHAF filings 
confirm the Cameron Lane five-year development deadline expired August 2025 with 
zero development initiated, eliminating any "defer until redevelopment" defense.

VIII. EACH DEFENDANT'S MOTION SHOULD BE DENIED 
1. City of Huntington Beach (ECF 117) 
The City directly owned and operated the HBNC, acquiring it through the Housing 
Authority before completing environmental review (Resolution No. 06, April 6, 2020, 
approved 7-0). City Manager Oliver Chi personally certified approximately $2.26 
million in CARES Act expenditures (CFDA #21.019) while internal documents 
showed contemporaneous knowledge of hazardous materials. City contact Steve 
Bogart was CC'd on OCHCA Supervising Specialist Geniece Higgins's February 
2022 email (Exhibit G-1) explicitly characterizing the HBNC closure as "closed with 
residual contamination left in place"—giving the City institutional knowledge that the 
NFA was a use-conditioned deferral. The City is a RCRA "person" under 42 U.S.C. § 
6903(15) who "contributed to" the conditions presenting endangerment.  
“Rule 12(b)(6) sufficiency” conclusion: 
These allegations easily satisfy Twombly/Iqbal’s plausibility standard.

4. RWQCB/Eric Lindberg (ECF 121) 
Lindberg's sovereign immunity defense fails for three reasons, each sufficient on its 
own. Plaintiff does not rely on Lapides v. Bd. of Regents, 535 U.S. 613 (2002), which 
addressed waiver by removal—a fact pattern not present here. Instead, Plaintiff relies 
on the Ex parte Young exception and on the mandatory, ministerial nature of 
Lindberg's duty under California law. 
(a) Lindberg's Duty Is Mandatory, Not Discretionary 
Case 8:26-cv-00348-JWH-ADS     Document 130     Filed 09/09/26     Page 21 of 373   Page

The threshold question under Ex parte Young is whether Plaintiff seeks prospective 
relief against a state official for an ongoing violation of federal law. Va. Office for 
Prot. & Advocacy v. Stewart, 563 U.S. 247, 255 (2011). Here, the duty is mandatory. 
Cal. Water Code § 13304 states that the RWQCB "shall issue a cleanup order" when 
waste is discharged in violation of the law. This is mandatory language— "shall," not 
"may." When Plaintiff presented documented Cr(VI) at 0.330 ȝg/L and 0.4 ȝg/L, and 
lead at 18 ȝg/L, Lindberg had no discretion to decline to act. His failure to issue a 
Cleanup and Abatement Order is not a discretionary policy choice; it is an ongoing 
violation of a mandatory statutory duty. 
Because the duty is ministerial, the Ex parte Young exception applies without the 
comity concerns that arise when a federal court is asked to direct a state official's 
exercise of discretion.  
In addition: 
x California Sportfishing Protection Alliance v. State Water Resources 
Control Board, 160 Cal. App. 4th 1625, 1639–1640 (2008) held that 
traditional mandamus under Cal. Code Civ. Proc. § 1085 lies to compel a 
water board's execution of mandatory, non-discretionary regulatory 
parameters;  
x Sierra Club v. Regional Water Quality Control Board, 205 Cal. App. 3d 
585, 593 (1988) found that traditional mandamus is the proper vehicle to 
Case 8:26-cv-00348-JWH-ADS     Document 130     Filed 09/09/26     Page 22 of 373   Page

challenge a regional board's failure to perform an absolute, mandatory 
environmental obligation;  
x Monterey Coastkeeper v. State Water Resources Control Board, 28 Cal. 
App. 5th 342, 361 (2018) clarified that while an agency has latitude in 
choosing its enforcement mechanisms, it lacks the discretion to ignore or 
completely waive explicit statutory triggers when a violation is clear.  
(b)  Ex parte Young Applies 
Ex parte Young, 209 U.S. 123 (1908), permits suits for prospective injunctive relief 
against state officials who are violating federal law. Plaintiff seeks precisely that: an 
order requiring Lindberg to exercise his independent authority under Cal. Water Code 
§ 13304 to issue a Cleanup and Abatement Order. This is not a claim for damages; it 
is a claim for prospective relief against a state official's ongoing failure to perform a 
mandatory duty. See Stewart, 563 U.S. at 255. 
(c) Lindberg Holds Direct Permit-Specific Authority 
WDID 8 30W004769 is a specific permit. Lindberg's authority is not generic 
supervisory authority; it is identified, permit-bound authority that he affirmatively 
declined to exercise. This distinguishes his situation from cases where the plaintiff 
seeks to compel a state official to exercise broad discretionary authority over an 
entire regulatory program. Lindberg's duty runs specifically to this Site, under this 
permit, and he has declined to perform it. 
“Rule 12(b)(6) sufficiency” conclusion:

The MOU proves the endangerment is ongoing, not historical. The contamination 
was never remediated—it was merely deferred to a redevelopment timeline that 
expired in August 2025 with zero development initiated. The engineering controls

--- PAGE 31 ---
Kevin Coker 
October 8, 2024 
Page 2 
 
site, with some ancillary improvements occurring along Finley Avenue. After reviewing 
the project, DTSC recommends and requests consideration of the following comments: 
1. In Table 5.2, HAZ-3, Me thod of Verification column of the Final ISMND, it 
states: “Provide a copy of the Soil and Groundwater Management Plan 
(SGMP) to the Santa Rosa Fire Department (SRFD) for review and approval. 
Retain copy of the approved document on-site during construction”. DTSC 
does not recommend that a SGMP, or a Soil Management Plan (SMP), be 
used as a primary cleanup plan to address contamination within the soil 
present at the site. DTSC recommends that any potential contamination be 
fully characterized and then remediated under the oversight of a self-certified 
local agency DTSC or Regional Water Quality Control Board (RWQCB). A 
SMP or SGMP alone cannot sufficiently identify and document the potential 
contaminants that may pose a threat to human health and the environment. 
DTSC recommends that a cleanup plan, a Removal Action Workplan (RAW) 
or Remedial Action Plan (RAP), be prepared to adequately address all site 
impacts after complete characterization. DTSC also recommends the City of 
Santa Rosa to coordinate with RWQCB to determine if the Groundwater 
portion of the recommended SGMP is sufficient to address the groundwater 
concerns. 
2. DTSC recommends the City of Santa Ro sa enter into a voluntary agreement 
to address contamination at brownfields and other types of properties or 
receive oversight from a self-certified local agency, DTSC or Regional Water 
Quality Control Board. If entering into one of DTSC’s voluntary agreements, 
please note that DTSC uses a single standard Request for Lead Agency 
Oversight Application for all agreement types. Please apply for DTSC 
oversight using this link: Request for Agency Oversight Application. Submittal 
of the online application includes an agreement to pay costs incurred during 
agreement preparation. If you have any questions about the application 
portal, please contact your Regional Brownfield Coordinator.

Kevin Coker 
October 8, 2024 
Page 3 
 
3. DTSC recommends that all imported soil and fill material should be tested to 
assess any contaminants of concern meet screening levels as outlined in 
DTSC's Preliminary Endangerment Assessment (PEA) Guidance Manual. 
Additionally, DTSC advises referencing the DTSC Information Advisory Clean 
Imported Fill Material Fact Sheet if importing fill is necessary. To minimize the 
possibility of introducing contaminated soil and fill material there should be 
documentation of the origins of the soil or fill material and, if applicable, 
sampling be conducted to ensure that the imported soil and fill material are 
suitable for the intended land use. The soil sampling should include analysis 
based on the source of the fill and knowledge of the prior land use. Additional 
information can be found by visiting DTSC’s Human and Ecological Risk Office 
(HERO) webpage. 
4. If buildings or other structures ar e to be demolished on any project sites 
included in the proposed project, surveys should be conducted for the presence 
of lead-based paints or products, mercury, asbestos containing materials, and 
polychlorinated biphenyl caulk. Removal, demolition, and disposal of any of the 
above-mentioned chemicals should be conducted in compliance with California 
environmental regulations and policies. In addition, sampling near current and/or 
former buildings should be conducted in accordance with DTSC’s Preliminary 
Endangerment Assessment (PEA) Guidance Manual

protecting California’s people and environment from the harmful effects of toxic 
substances. If you have any questions or would like clarification on DTSC’s comments, 
please respond to this letter or via email for additional guidance.

1.0 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
Integrity Environmental consultants, Jnc. (Integrity) was retained by City Of Huntington Beach <Cityl to 
provide envlronmental services consisting Of four <41 consecutive tasks for the City Hall complex !Site> 
located at 2000 Main street in City Of Huntington Beach, Callfornla: 11 Response to an emergency related to 
worker exposure to uncovered Coal Tar creosote {Creosotel and Creosote-contaminated material during 
the Site seismic retrOfitting project; 21 prepare and Implement a site-specific health and safety plan (HASPI, 
perform llmlted site assessment/background study, present a collective site characterization; 3l conduct a 
human health risk assessment, evaluate feaslblllty Of remedlal alternatlves, prepare a Remedial Action 
workplan <RAW) for the site; and 4l perform visual observation and documentation Of remedial activities 
during Implementation Of the RAW, enforce health and safety requirements according to HASP, confirm the 
post remediation surface clean-up, and prepare a Remedial Actions and Closure Report. 
Three tasks were performed under supervision Of Department Of Toxic Substance control <DTSCI. Data 
collected at the conclusion Of each task was presented In Interim Progress Report-1, Progress Report -2 and 
the Site RAW, reports Of which tasks had been submitted to DTSC. The closure report summarizes the Site 
activities with respect to details Of remediation, confirmation Of Post-Remediation surface cleanup and 
request for the Site Closure based on collective conclusions. Key points in conducting the site Investigation 
and remediation are presented In the followlng subsections. 
1.1 Pre-Remediation Period 
A site contamination assessment was Initially performed for the City ln response to an outbreak Of 
dermatitis among unprotected construction workers removing creosote from the building basement In 
June 2008. In the 1970s, the creosote was used as waterproofing material and primer to attach sidings on 
the subsurface exterior walls of the building basement and foundation which material were backfilled with 
soils and covered concrete slabs. The removal Of concrete slab, excavation Of soils and removal of the 
Creosote was a part of the Seismic Upgrade Project at the Site. Upon discovery of Creosote, the excavated 
areas and materials were covered temporarily with polyethylene sheeting to mlnimite further exposures. 
on behalf Of the City, DTSC became Involved in the site remediation. DTSC assigned a project team to 
maintain the Site supervision and provide directions and advice to the City. DTSC required the site 
remediation to be conducted under the DTSC-approved RAW. Integrity conducted Best Management 
Practices (BMPsl for protection and prevention of contamination proliferation at the Site. As a part of BMPs 
and to comply with occupational Safety and Health Administration <OSHAI, OSHA-mandated HAZWOPER 
training was mandated for workers performing construction and remediation work activities at the Site. 
Progress Report 1 documented part Of the site characterization performed under DTSC's oversight and 
approved Site sampling Plan. Bulk soil samples were obtained from work Area 4, background soil and trailer 
staging area piled soil. Bulk soil samples were analyzed for volatile organic compounds <VOCsl, semi-volatile 
organic compounds (SVOCsl and polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons <PAHsl. Integrity also performed DTSC· 
approved soil gas sampling. The reported analytlcal results showed low concentrations of none-site-related 
voes. 
Progress Report-2 provided additional data needed for site characterization, which included waste stream 
sampling, soll and lateral soil contamination sampllng, Indoor and outdoor air sampling for svocs and PAHs, 
outdoor voes sampling, indoor and outdoor wlpe sampling for svocs and PAHs. 
Air samples were collected to evaluate the Impacts of the construction activity and to assess existing 
conditions of the Indoor and outdoor air quallty at the Site. Air sampling was performed In accordance with 
the approved Site Sampling Plan. Air samples were collected from various locations and were analyzed for 
voes and PAHs using EPA Methods T0-15 and T0-13A, respectively. Air samples were addltlonally tested for 
Naphthalene and 2-Methylnaphthalene using EPA Method T0-17. The analytical results indicated that 
concentrations of voes, and PAHs Uncluding Naphthalene and 2-Methylnaphthalenel were Insignificant.

Indoor and outdoor wipe sampling was conducted for hard surfaces in accordance with ASTM Method o-
6661-01. For other unpaved areas around the buildings, shallow soil samples were collected at seven <7l 
locations following the surface soil sampling method recommended by Army corps of Engineers. 
oust dispersion study was also conducted at the same time and conceptual models were prepared for 
generation, veloclty and distribution of dust cloud over the excavation area and movement and deposition 
of dust particles. For the purpose of our conceptual model, a plot was prepared for Benzo<aJpyrene 
concentrations !for wipe and shallow soil samples> and based on a prominent west-southwest wind report 
for the area. At the conclusion, It was speculated that It would be less likely that dust particles, generated as 
a southerly moving dust clouds, to migrate to northerly-situated main entrance of Administration Building. 
eased on the collected data and In accordance with the site characterization, the chemicals of concern 
(COCJ, the source and distribution and background levels of the coc were established. A conceptual model 
was prepared and the various scenarios for current and future receptors for the site, their exposure 
pathway and duration of exposure were animated. Using Tier 1 Human Health screening Level, the 
prellmlnarv human health risk for each scenario was evaluated. The coc were reduced to carcinogenic PAHs 
through dermal contact and ingestion for soil and fugitive dust particles and Naphthalene as vapor phase 
through inhalation. 
Using DTSC-approved models, the human health risk for the existing site use as City Hall was calculated. The 
cleanup criterion of PAHs In soil, soil vapor, and dust particle Is evaluated on a risk-based concentrations to 
mitigate exposure to carcinogen and non-carcinogen PAHs to achieve the risk levels of <10"

for City 
employees, <10"

for City visitors, <10"

5 
for Offslte residents, 
school attendees and pedestrians. 
A feasibility study was conducted with the participation Of the DTSC-asslgned Project Team. Removal action 
alternatives were evaluated for their effectiveness, Implementability, and cost. Given the condition of the 
site and the result of the risk assessment, combination of selective excavation, partial removal of the 
creosote coating, coverage and fixation of treated surfaces, backfill of the excavated area, placement Of 
vapor barrier and concrete cap was selected as the final remedial alternative and was further evaluated for 
compliance with the Applicable or Relevant and Appropriate ReQulrements. 
1.2 During-Remediation Period 
Remediation of creosote and creosote-contaminated materials were required in six (61 areas, referred to 
work Areas 1 through 6. castlerock Environmental Initiated the remedial action in late December 2008, and 
completed the remedial actions on April 9, 2009. All work was perfOrmed In accordance with the DTSC· 
approved RAW using hazmat trained workers with proper personal protection equipment. 
During this period, after securing the work zones, Work Areas 2 through 6 were excavated. concrete was 
cut and creosote membrane and associated waste were removed. The excavated materials were 
segregated, bagged and placed In secure storage bins. The excavated soil was placed over polyethylene 
sheeting and the stoci<plled materials were covered by polyethylene sheeting. Protillng was perfOrmed on 
the waste stream to categorize the materials prior to transportation Of the waste to an appropriate disposal 
faclllty. The excavated areas were properly detailed and cleaned and covered with protective and new 
waterprootlng materials as a part Of Seismic Upgrade Project. 
Approximately 247 cubic yards of creosote. and Creosote-containing or impacted material and 11 55-gallon 
drums of contaminated soil and decontamination water were transported by BOC Services, using proper 
manifestation procedures to Chemical Waste Management - KFH, In Kettleman City, California. 
Integrity staff performed dally visual observation and documentation of the remediation activities. worker 
and perimeter air sampling, heat stress and noise monitoring and real time measurement of dust emission 
were performed on as needed basis. Data collected was used to maintain health and safety of the workers, 
onslte and offsite recipients bY correction of the safety level and maintaining safe work condition during 
remediation and backfilling operations in and around Work Areas. 
Integrity also inspected the placement Of vapor barrier and concrete cap In work Areas 1 through 6.

1.3 Post-Remediation sampling 
As a part Of the site BMPs, dally cleanup activities were performed In and around the work areas. Hard 
surface areas were cleaned up using sweeping equipment to remove the dust particles generated during 
excavation and backfill activities. The unpaved areas north and south Of the excavation area were cleaned 
up using HEPA-filtered vacuum cleaners. Confirmation air, wipe and soll samples were collected by Integrity 
to confirm the efficiency Of Post-Remedial cleanup activities: 
o Results Of outdoor air sampling for voes indicated low concentrations of different voes in the samples. 
The voes were not a part of coc, established for the Site and the low concentration levels for the voes 
may have been related to exposure to non-site related chemicals; 
o Results of outdoor air sampling for 16 targeted PAHs Indicated not detected above their respective 
laboratory method reporting levels !MRLs>; 
o Results Of outdoor air sampling for Naphthalene and 2·Methylnaphthalene Indicated that detected 
levels were In the same range as detected levels In the outdoor air Of other cities In the United states, 
reported by Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) and Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease 
Registry (ATSDR); 
o Results of Indoor air sampling for 16 targeted PAHs Indicated not detected above their respective MRLs; 
o Results Of indoor air sampling for Naphthalene and 2-Methylnaphthalene indicated that detected levels 
were In the same range as detected levels In the Indoor air of other Cities in the United states as 
reported by EPA and ATSDR. Even though the concentrations of these compounds exceeded the 
benchmark of 0.12 microgram per cubic meter, nevertheless Is within the range Of their background 
levels; • 
o Results Of outdoor wipe sampling for svocs revealed Bis (2-Ethylhexyn Phthalate as the only compound 
detected in the outdoor wipe samples. This compound Is not a part Of COC, established for the Site and 
since It was not detected In the outdoor air sample, the low concentration level of this svocs In the 
wipe sample Is not considered a significant risk factor; 
o Results Of outdoor wipe sampling for 16 targeted PAHs revealed the presence Of several PAHs In the 
samples above their respective method detection limits <MDLsl. The total PAHs In any of the wipe 
samples did not exceed the Benchmark Of 150 microgram per square meter; 
o Results of Indoor wipe sampling for svocs revealed the presence Of Bis <2-Ethvlhexyl) Phthalate and 
Butyl Benzyl Phthalate above their respective MDLs. These svocs were not a part Of coc, established for 
the Site and because they were not detected In the Indoor air sample. Low concentrations of these 
svocs in wipe sample are not considered a significant risk factor; 
o Results Of indoor wipe sampling for 16 targeted PAHs revealed that the compounds were not detected 
above their respective MDLs; 
o Shallow soil sampling results revealed that several PAHs were detected above their respective MDLs.

2.0 INTRODUCTION 
2.1 Purpose 
Integrity Environmental consultants, Inc. (Integrity) was retained by the City Of Huntington Beach -
Department Of Publlc works <City> to perform site remediation observation and documentation and Post­
Remediation air, dust wipe and shallow confirmation soil sampllng and prepared this final report (Report) 
for the City Hall site located at 2000 Main Street In City Of Huntington Beach, Callfornla <Sitel. The Report Is 
prepared for the work that was completed In accordance with the callfornia Health and Safety Code 
<CAH&SCl Chapter 6.8: Hazardous substances Account. 
2.2 Objectives 
The objectives Of this report were to summarize the results Of site investigation , site characterization and 
implementation Of the Remedial Action Workplan <RAW) for the Site. To date, Integrity has completed the 
following tasks: 
o Narration of the site characterization; 
o Preparation Of a feasibility study; 
o Preparation Of the RAW; 
o Development Of a site-specific Health and Safety Plan !HASPJ; 
o Monitoring Of remediation activities; 
o Post-remediation sampling; and 
o ReQuest for site closure. 
2.3 Site Location and Description 
The Site Is an approximately 11.81 acres municipal property located at 2000 Main Street In the City Of 
Huntington Beach, Callfornra. The Site Is developed on an 011 field Jn a mixed residential and commercial 
zone and Is bordering Yorktown Avenue to the north with commercial buildings beyond that, Lake Street to 
the east and residential properties beyond that , Park Street and portion of West Utica Avenue to the south 
with resldentlal properties beyond that, and Main street to the west and Huntington Beach High School 
across Main Street (Sheet SP1 of SP3 - Site Vicinity Mapl. 
The Site elevation Is approximately 70 feet above mean sea level !MSU at the northern property line and 
approximately so feet MSL at southern property llne. The surface topography has a gentle slope to the 
south. The existing structures at the Site include four (4) butldlngs, an active oil well, a mechanical oil pump 
and well production accessories, private streets, parking lot, paved area, planters, and recreational areas 
(Sheet SP2 Of SP3 - Site Aerial Photograph). 
2.4 Site GeOlogy 
The Site Is located along the easternmost edge Of the Huntington Beach Mesa, the western side Of the 
northern portion of the Santa Ana oap and south Of the active Newport-Inglewood Afqulst-Prlolo 
EarthQuake Fault zone. A number of domal hills and mesas exist parallel the Newport-Inglewood Fault zone 
as a result Of antlcllnal up-warping, uplifting, right lateral strike slip and compression shortening across the 
fault zone. The site is located within the Huntington Oil Fleld, which Is one Of these domal features. 
Erosion Of the uplifted areas within the Santa Ana oap by the Santa Ana River resulted In an angular 
unconformity between the Pleistocene aged sediments comprising the Talbert Aciulfer and a number Of 
older, underlying aQuifers. The mapped Holocene aged aqultard, above the Talbert AQulfer or alluVlal fan 
sediments, lap onto the late Pleistocene marine terrace deposits that cover the Huntington Beach and 
Newport mesas. The surface llmlts of the aquitard above the Talbert Aquifer define the Santa Ana Pressure 
Area Of the orange county Groundwater Basin. The aciultard above the Talbert AQulfer Inhibits significant 
quantities Of surface water and shallow groundwater from percolatlng Into the major producing aQulfers.

Along the coast, the absence Of the overlylng aqultard above the Talbert Aquifer, allows ocean water 
(seawater Intrusion> to percolate Into the Talbert Aquifer and eventually Into some of the underlying older 
aquifers that are In fault contact or angular unconformable contact with the Talbert Aquifer. Another 
contributing factor to seawater Intrusion Includes groundwater mining from a number Of major aquifers 
underlying the Talbert Aquifer, thereby altering the equilibrium between the ocean and fresh groundwater 
causing the Inland advancement Of saline water . 
The 1966 Groundwater Basin Protection Project, SaUnlty Barrier studies within the Santa Ana Gap was 
conducted and Implemented to protect main groundwater production aquifers from seawater Intrusion. A 
number of Intrusion wells were Installed along Ellis Avenue, the approximate southern boundary of where 
the Talbert Aquifer is In hydraulic continuity with maJor aquifers that supply the region's groundWater. The 
wells lnJect treated and fresh water Into underlying aquifers creating a barrier to further seawater Intrusion. 
The Talbert Aquifer in the general area has been Identified at approximately 70 feet below ground surface 
Cbgs> with the main production aquifers being located at approximately 130 feet bgs, although production 
wells are located north of the seawater barrier. Within the site vicinity, near-surface, semi-perched 
groundwater ts of poor quality and Is not used for production. 
2.5 Site History 
Based on data provided by the City, coal Tar creosote <creosote> was used during construction of the Clty 
Hall buildings In 1970's. creosote was used as waterproofing material and a primer to attaeh sidings on 
subsurface of the building exterior walls. The waterproOfed surfaces were, subsequently backfilled by soil 
and finished with concrete slabs. construction activities for City Hall Seismic Upgrade Project- cash contract 
Number 1306; required uncovering the Creosote in six <6> areas, hereafter are referred to Work Areas 1 
through 6 <Sheet SP3 of SP3 - Site Work Areas>. 
Integrity visited and Inspected the Site on June 24, 2008 at the request of the City to evaluate an Incident In 
which six <6> construction workers who had performed scraping and manual removal of the creosote 
without personal protective equipment, developed dermatitis on their hands, arms and neck area and were 
hospitalized. construction activities were halted and the creosote waste that was generated by these 
workers was bagged loosely and covered with polyethylene sheeting at the construction trailer staging area 
on the south side Of the Site. 
3.0 EMERGENCY SITE EVALUATION 
3.1 Initial Site Visit and Inspection 
Integrity Investigated contractor workers becoming ill and developing skin rash as they manually removed 
Creosote from a construction area known as the pit immediately adjacent to the buildings on the west side 
entrance. Representatives of Integrity and the City conducted a walk-through Of the excavated areas and 
the staging area on June 24, 2008. The staging area had been established on the south side Of bulldlngs 
where the removed creosote was stored . The site configuration and positioning of the creosote and their 
close proximity to the buildings entrances were noted. Loose storage of the Creosote and a need to sample 
removed material and excavated soil <to chemically characterize the Creosote and determine the extent of 
soll contamination> were deemed as a flrst step for the Site hazard evaluation and characterization. 
3.2 Initial sampling of Site Contamination 
Integrity collected the first group of samples consisting of one 111 sample of In-place Creosote and one <1> 
sample of potentially contaminated sol! on June 25, 2008. The samples were submitted on June 26, 2008 to 
American Environmental Testing Laboratory <American Testing Laboratory> In Burbank, California. The 
samples were analyzed for semi-Volatile Organic Vapors <svocsl and Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons 
IPAHsl in accordance with the United states Environmental Protection Agency <EPA) SW-846 - Test Methods 
for Evaluating Solfd Waste, PhVSlcal/Chemlcal Method. Method 8270C and Method 8310 were used for the 
analysis of svocs and PAHs, respectively. The tests were pertormed to determine whether the matrices can 
be classified as Resource conservation and Recovery Act (RCRAl wastes and/or California ·non-RCRA wastes.

northerly-situated main entrance.

7.1 Human Health Risk Assessment 
Based on the collected data and in accordance with the site characterization, coc and the source and 
distribution and background levels Of the coc were established. A conceptual model was prepared and the 
various scenarios for current and future receptors for the site, their exposure pathway and durat ion of 
exposure were animated. Using Tier 1 Human Health Screening Level, the preliminary human health risk for 
each scenario was evaluated. The coc were reduced to carcinogenic PAHs through dermal contact and 
Ingestion for soil and fugitive dust particles and Naphthalene as vapor phase through inhalation. 
Using DTSC approved models; the human health risk for the existing site use as city hall was calculated. The 
cleanup criterion of PAHs as In soil, soll vapor, and dust particle Is evaluated on a risk-based concentrat ions 
to mitigate exposure to carcinogen and non-carcinogen PAHs to achieve the risk levels of <10"

for the City 
employees, to <10"
5 
for the City visitors, to <10"
5 
for future landscapers and utility workers, to <10"5 for offslte

7.2 Feaslbtllty of Removal Altemattves 
A feasiblllty study was conducted with the participation of the Project Team. Removal action alternatives 
were evaluated for their effectiveness, implementability, and cost. Given the condition of the site the risk 
assessment, combination of selective excavation, partial removal Of the creosote coating, coverage and 
fixation Of treated surfaces, backflll Of the excavated area, placement Of vapor barrier and concrete cap was 
selected as the final remedial alternative and was further evaluated tor compliance with the ARARs.

The RAW was prepared In compliance with the requirement Of DTSC and recommendation of the Project 
Team for mitigation of PAHs bearing Creosote and Creosote contaminated materials. The RAW lnduded a

summary site characterization, a human health risk assessment, evaluation of feasibility of the remedial options,

9.1.1 Delineation of Excavation Areas 
Prior to remediation activity, the Site was divided Into three !3) zones: Exclusion zone, Contamination 
Reduction zone and support zone. The Exclusion zone was Isolated from the rest Of the Site to prevent 
entry Of unauthorized people. The Exclusion zone was fenced and equipped with proper slgnage, readable 
from at least 25 feet. This zone was moved as the work activities moved to cover the area known or 
suspected to have contaminant at concentrations exceeding the remedial goal. Individual work zones 
within the Excluslon zone, where contaminated material had been excavated or stockpiled, were cordoned 
Off prior to the initiation Of remediation, and Ingress and egress from these areas was controlled. 
A Contamination Reduction zone was delineated at the entrance Of the Exclusion zone to allow for removal 
Of dust, debris and soil from equipment, transportation vehldes and personnel leaving the Exduslon Zone. 
Decontamination methods consisted Of brushing or high-pressure washing, depending on weather 
conditions, during the remedial activities. The Contamination Reduction zone was large enough to 
accommodate steam cleaning or pressure washing Of all eQulpment. Personnel decontamination also took 
place as workers left the work zones. The location and size Of the contamination Reduction zone for 
personnel changed as the Site conditions and operations dictated. 
The support zone tl.e., safe zone> was establlshed upwind of the contamination Reduction zone. All 
equipment and personnel entering the support zone were properly decontaminated prior to entering this 
zone. The support zone was set up onslte prior to the start Of remedial work. This area provided for 
administrative and support functions «command post, first aid station, rest area, drinking facility, etc.> 
necessary to keep the field activities operating smoothly. Potable water and wash facilities were provided 
for the field personnel In this location.

Effective means of dust and erosion control were utilized to minimize the generation of dust and erosion 
associated with excavation activities, truck and vehicle traffic onto and Off the Site and the effects of 
ambient wind traversing exposed soil. Engineering controls, used to mitigate dust levels and erosion were 
as fallows: 
o Misting or spraying water during soil excavation and loading Of soil transportation vehicles; 
o Limiting vehicle speeds to 5 miles per hour on unpaved portions Of the Site; 
o covering stockpiled soil with plastic sheeting; 
o Minimizing drop heights of soil during loadlng/unloadlng activities; 
o Minimizing (optimizing water> used In dust control; and 
o Rapid cleanup of sediments deposited on paved roadways.

9.2 Remediation Activities and Dally oversight 
Metro BUilders had Inadvertently excavated at Work Areas 1 and 2 and stockpiled the material at the location 
of the excavation. The excavated materials were subJect to dispersion by gusty winds prior to Integrity's 
Involvement on June 24, 2008. During the emergency response and site characterization phase, Integrity 
profiled the excavated Creosote; Creosote-containing stockpiled soil and concrete. Based on the analytical 
results of profiling samples, the excavated materials were segregated and transferred to the southeast 
corner Of the Site for temporary storage. 
The remedial activities were performed at six (6l isolated work areas !Work Area 1 through Work Area 6). The 
location, shape and dimensions of the excavated areas were not controlled by the level of soil 
contamination or lateral and vertical migration of the contaminant. on the contrary, the only controlling 
factor was the design of retrofitting plan and the area needed to be uncovered or excavated to flt the 
reinforcement concrete footings , Therefore, remediation at each work Area was limited to removal and 
disposal of creosote and sofl, sand or vermlcullte in contact with the waterprOOfing layer. Access for 
reinforcement Of the building In worl< Areas 1, 2, and 4 required excavations and removal of native soil 
around the footings of buildings, whlle In Work Areas 3, 5, and 6 small sections of concrete slab were cut 
around the reinforcing columns without encountering any soil. Two (2) layers of vermiculite and sand which 
separated the creosote layer from the concrete cap were removed. 
The Creosote and vermiculite waste were placed In double 10-mll polyethylene bags and stored In closed 
space metal container far disposal as callfarnfa Hazardous Waste. Based on the analytical results of the soil 
in Immediate contact with creosote as well as concrete blocks In direct contact with creosote were 
considered contaminated and were placed In a separate bin far disposal as Cafffarnla Non-hazardous waste. 
The analytical results of profiling samples collected from the stockpiled soll, Indicated that the 
concentration of carcinogenic PAHs, presented as Benzo!a>pyrene eqUfvalent IB<alPI, was below the average 
background level in the area. 
As discussed in site characterization and the RAW, detailed evaluation of analytical results of soil samples 
collected from excavation areas as well as the soil samples collected from stockpiled soil, Indicated that the 
impact Of long-term presence of Creosote on the adjacent soll was mlnlmal and the average PAHs 
concentration of these soil samples Is below the average background level of PAHs far the Site (as an 
ollfieldl. Lack of percolating water beneath the slab and concrete cover, and low solubllity of the PAHs 
compounds are the contrlb1,1tlng factors to this condition. Additionally, with the consideration Of the 
calculated risk far the selected remedial alternative and depth to groundwater the proposed alternative of 
using the stockpile soit for backfill of the excavation pits was Justified and with concurrence of the Project 
Team and approval of DTSC, this soil was later used as backfill material in excavation Work Areas 1, 2, and 4.

trained Metro Builders workers began backfilling Of the excavation pit using the soil transported from the

layer was indiVidua!ly compacted and tested . Compaction tests were performed , soil density was tested and 
achievement Of the desired density was approved by HAZWOPER-trained Oeotech or smith-Emery technicians 
equipped with personal protection equipment lndudlng respiratory protection gear. 
In order to control dust dispersion levels, a real-time measurement Of wind velocity and dust partlcles were 
misted w ith water for dust suppression during loading, backfill and compaction process. 
on February 27, Metro Builders workers completed the cleanup Of this Work Area and prepared the topsoil . A 
two-inch layer of sand was placed on the topsoil; two (2l layers Of 10-mil polyethylene sheets were placed on the 
sand as the vapor barrier and covered with another two-Inch layer Of sand. The vapor barrier was capped with a 
four -Inch layer of concrete at the surface level to serve as the engineered cap per the approved RAW.

9.2.7 Additional sou Remediation 
During the unsupervised excavation fn Work Area 2, dispersion of the fugitive dust particles created a new 
source of contamination . Refer to Figures: Sheets Wind 1 and 2 of Wind 4 - KCAHUBT116 weather Graph for 
2008, Sheet Wind 3 of Wind 4 - Wind south-Southwesterly Direction and Sheet Wind 4 of Wind 4 -Wind West­
southwesterly Direction. As Illustrated, the controlling factors In locality of the settled dust are wind 
direction , wind velocity and configuration of the building structures. The Increased velocity of the wind 
between the buildings onslte carries dust partldes to the open area where they WIii settle and wlll be 
deposited as fan with the slmllar concept as alluvial fan after exiting from a gorge. The main portion of the 
settled dust created an apron southeast of the police department building. This phenomenon (Illustrated In 
Figure: (Sheet s1 of s2 - Final Confirmation SOIi Sampling Locations> as distribution pattern of BlalP 
concentration with the highest concentration depicted In sample LC-1 located at the front llne of the dust 
particle deposition fan. Although this sample was the only sample with B ◄ a>P concentration slightly 
exceeding the average background levels, It triggered the requirement for a Post-Remediation surface 
clean-up followed by a repeated sampling event to confirm the adequacy of final surface cleanup. 
The recommended surface soil sampling was performed on August 26, 2009. Presence of Ben:zo<aJpyrene 
and B<a>P equivalent at confirmation sample cs-1 at the same location of the original surface sample LC-1 
(refer to Section 4.6 of this report! required addltlonal soll sampling to delineate the lateral and vertical 
extent of the Impact on shallow soll at this location. Based on the results for the samples collected on 
November 16, 2009; addltlonal soil remediation became necessary for shallow Impacted soll and as the result 
of dust dispersion. 
An addendum to the approved RAW for the soil remediation at the subject locality was discussed with the 
Project Team, presented to DTSC and approved by DTSC on December 9, 2009 <References - Item 231. A copy 
of addendum to the approved RAW as well as DTSC acceptance is presented In Appendix M - status of 
Remediation Actions Report. 
Under direct supervision of DTSC, as observed and documented by Integrity, remediat ion of shallow soil at 
this location stared on January 11, 2010. The limits of excavation area were marked on the ground. During 
an onsite meeting, the scope Of work and health and safety Issues were presented to and discussed with 
castlerock Environmental personnel. Perimeter of the excavation area was covered by polyethylene 
sheeting to prevent further distribution of soil and dust. Using a BobcatTM, the upper six-Inches Of Impacted 
soil at this location was stripped placed In four <41 55-gallon DOT-approved drums and covered bin. The 
drums were then labeled and manifested with generator and site information , content , date and nature of 
contamination, and transported to chemical waste treatment facility In Kettleman City, California under 
proper manifestation.

9.7 Heat stress Management 
Heat stress measurements were perfOrmed on as-needed bases during the period of December 30, 2008 
through April 9, 2009 In compliance with cal/OSHA Heat Illness Prevention <8 CCR 33951 and the HASP 
requirements. Heat Illness Is a serious matter that cannot be Ignored. Every year there are many serious 
injuries Including death as a result of heat stress related conditions. Having the tools to monitor, track and 
take preventative measures Is a key to addressing what some consider a difficult problem to Identify. 
Thermal load of the human body sensed in a room or outdoor s i s significantly Influenced by four (41 
environmental characteristics : air temperature , humidity, radiant heat, and wind speed. The Wet Bulb Globe 
Temperature IWBOTl Index combines three (31 thermal factors ; humidity, radiant heat and ambient 
temperature In single Indicator of heat stress called the WBOT Index. The WBGT Index is commonly used as a 
guide for environmental heat stress measurements to prevent heat stress during work in hot 
environments . The WBGT Index measurements were recorded using Reuter-stokes™ WBGT meter. WBGT meter 
recorded readings were In excess of 80°F. The guidance for WBGT data application Is proVlded by the American 
Conference of Governmental Industrial Hygienists !ACOIH> documented In the Threshold Umlt Values fOr 
Chemic.al Substanres and Chem/cal Agents and Bfo/ogica/ Exposure Ind/res, 2006. ACOIH Indicated that for the use 
of air tight protective garment, e.g. saranexllll, the WBOT reading Is adjusted by adding 20°F whidl would raise 
that high WBOT reading to 100°F or higher. As a result workers were given more breaks than normal to replenish 
fluids and ensure enough cooling which prevented the development of the serious heat stress symptoms and 
conditions.

10.0 POST-REMEDIATION SAMPLING METHODOLOGY 
10.1 Sampling Dates 
Post-remediation sampling took place during the following dates in August through November 2009: 
o There was no remediation activities for the month of August 2009; 
o The site was Inspected on August 11, 2009 by the HASP - Health and Safety Director and a representative Of 
the City In preparation for the post-remediation sampling; 
o A follow up site inspection was carried on August 20, 2009 to ensure adequate preparation and detailing Of 
work areas for post-remediation sampling; 
o Post-remediation indoor and outdoor wipe sampling was performed by Integrity on August 25, 2009; 
o Post-remediation Indoor and outdoor air sampling for PAHs was performed by Integrity on August 26, 2009; 
o Post-remediation Indoor and outdoor arr sampling for Naphthalene and Methylnaphthatene was performed 
by Integrity on August 27, 2009; 
o Post-remediation indoor and outdoor air sampling for voes was perfOrmed by Integrity on August 28, 2009; 
o Surface soll confirmation sampling was performed on August 26, 2009; and 
o Additional sou confirmation samples, to delineate the lateral and vertlcal extent of the Impact on 
shallow soll, were collected on November 16, 2009.

10.3 Pre-sampling Site Detailing and Sampling Preparation 
Integrity generated checklists for site detailing In preparation for Post-Remediation sampling as follows: 
o April a, 2009 - a site walk-through was performed by the HASP Health and Safety Director and progress 
of remediation and construction work visually observed. A checklist was generated to secure the 
regulated areas especially work Areas 1 and 4 and the Staging Area In anticipation of the final cleanup 
and Post-Remediation sampling. securing the work areas included the removal of construction debris 
and loose soll from surfaces, and covering work areas with plastic sheeting and securing entrances to 
the bulldlng . construction work Including carpentry, Iron work and concrete work continued during 
the period of April through August 2009, which did not allow a final cleanup in preparation for post­
remediation sampling. 
o July 29. 2009 - Integrity Professional Geologist Inspected the site on July 29, 2009 and Indicated that all is fine 
with work Areas 1 through 6 and the Trailer Staging Area still contains the 55-gallon drum containing 
decontamination water. 
o August 11. 2009 - a walkthrough Of the construction site was carried out jointly by the HASP Health and 
Safety Director and the City representative. It was noted that Work Areas 1 through 6 needed removal Of 
large debris and general cleanup to expose the existing slabs and prepare It for the wipe sampling. The 
staging Area and stairs needed removal of the clean sand and used black sandblasting sand from the 
steps, wood and fences and perform a thorough cleaning and washing Of steps. The Trailer staging 
Area needed removal Of remnants of the stored sol! used as fill in the area fn front Of the entrance 
facing the council Chambers. Once the soil was removed the area needs general cleanup followed by 
water and steam cleanlng to restore to original form. It was decided that another site walk-through be 
perfOrmed to ensure the site Is readied fOr post-remediation sampling. 
o August 20, 2009 - a final walkthrough Of the construction site was carried out jointly by the HASP Health 
and Safety Director and the City representative in which a great deal Of progress of cleanup and 
detailing was observed In work Areas 1 through 6, the staging Area and stairs and the Trailer staging 
Area. However, some cleaning and detailing remained to be completed. The City representative 
indicated that since there would be no construction activities at the present, Metro Builders would 
perform the final cleaning and detailing on August 22, 2009. The City representative further Indicated 
the City representative would visually Inspect the site to ensure It was cleaned and detailed thoroughly 
on August 24. 2009. As a result post-remediation sampling was schedUled for August 25, 2009.

was observed by DTSC;

was about five 15l feet above ground and collected alr tor a period of 8-9 hours. Locations of the samples 
and tr1p blank were phOtographlcaUy documented; and 
o At the end of the day, the canisters were collected, valves shut, capped and submitted with trip blank and a

10.4.2 Polycvcllc Aromatic Hydrocarbons Air sampling Method 
PAHs sampling was conducted In accordance with EPA Method T0·13A as follows: 
o sampllng was conducted at five 15) outdoor locations and 15 indoor locations In the Police Building, 
Administrative Building, Development Bulldlng and council Chambers on August 26, 2009. The sampling 
was observed by Mr. Safouh Sayed, Project Manager for DTSC; 
o Method TO·13A was modified to utilize low air flow rate filter and sorbent PUF/XAD-2 media glass tubes. 
The filter and sorbent tubes were prepared with XAD·2 sandwiched between layers Of PUF; 
o The PUF/XAD-2 glass tubes were prepared at Columbia Analytical and cleaned In solvent and vacuum 
dried in mid-August 2009 and received by Integrity onsite on August 25, 2009; 
o sKc• programmable air sampling suction pumps were used In this sampling and pre-sampling calibrated 
with manufacturer certified orvcai-, a primary electronic calibrator, at approximately three (3l liters per 
minute using a PUF/XAD·2 tube designated for callbratlon connected In series using rubber tubing on 
August 26, 2009; 
o The calibrated pumps were connected to the PUF/XAD·2 media tubes using 5-foot long Teflon tubing 
and were distributed at the 20 outdoor and Indoor sampling locations; 
o The PUF/XAD·2 tubes were removed from the capped protective Jars and the bubble wrap and 
aluminum foll wrap removed and attached to each pump at the location Of the sample and pumps were 
started ; 
o The height of the PUF/XAD·2 tubes was approximately the revel Of the average breathing zone Of a 
normal adult; 
o Two (2l PUF/XAD·2 media tubes were used as fleld blanks. The two tubes were opened and passively 
aired fOr 30 seconds and sealed; 
o Prior to the start of each sample, sampling trains were tested for leakage by simply pinching the tubing 
Of each train and If the pump stopped that Indicated no leakage. All the sampling trains passed the 
leakage test; 
o The sampling trains were monitored to ensure continuous and uninterrupted operation, and samples 
were run for 458 to 516 minutes depending on the sample. The sampling trains and iocatlons were 
photographically documented. None of the 20 air sampling pumps malfunctloned during this sampling;

10.s Indoor and Outdoor Surface Wipe sampllng 
Wipe samplJng was conducted In accordance with ASTM Method D-6661·01: Practice for Collect/on of Organic 
compounds from surfaces Using Wloo sampling. as follows: 
o Wipe sampling was performed on August 25, 2009 using two <21 100-square centimeters side-by-side 
wipe sampling areas; 
o The side-by-side sampling areas were marked Off using 100-square centimeters paper templates 
precisely adhered to the wipe area and utlllzed only once and discarded; 
o The sampling media consisted Of square gauze wipes prepared and cleaned by American Testing 
Laboratory prior to the sampling day, The gauze measured approxlmately 3-inches square in size and 
wetted either In two <2> mllltllters of de-Ionized water or n-hexane in 100-mllllliters capped jars; 
o The purpose of the two (2) different side-by-side wipes was to analyze the wipes wetted with n-hexane 
using EPA Method 8270C for svocs and the wipes wetted with de-ionized water using EPA Method 8310 
for PAHs; 
o Side-by-side wipe sampllng was performed at 25 Indoor locations In the Police Building, Administrative 
Building, Development Building and Council Chambers; 
o Side-by-side wipe sampling was performed at 25 outdoor locations Inside and outside work Areas 1 
through 6; 
o One <11 sampling location was wiped using de-Ionized water wetted gauze and the other wiped with 
hexane wetted gauze. Two <2> separate tweezers were used to handle the two <2> different gauzes out 
of their respective Jars, to perform the wipe action and place the wipe back into the Jar. Nltr11e• gloves 
were used between wipes to prevent cross contamination and the gauzes were never handled by hand 
during the wiping process; 
o Wipe motion consisted of gently wiping each square horizontally once and perpendlcularly once using 
the same wipe; 
o After the wipe of each square was completed , the gauze was Inserted back Into the respective Jar using 
the tweezers and capped, and wipes from each location were labeled with sample number; 
o The samples were preserved and chilled In a cooler with sufficient number of blue Ice packages and 
submitted with chain-Of-custody records to American Testing Laboratory at the end Of the sampling day 
for analysls; and 
o unused Hexane-wetted gauzes and de-lonlzed water wetted gauzes from the same sampling batches 
• were selected by American Testing Laboratory as field blanks for analysis.

10.6 Confirmation Shallow Soll sampllng 
10.6.1 Rationale for confirmation Shallow son sampllng 
oust partldes generated during the orlglnal uncontrolled excavation and transportation of PAH-impacted 
material was depleted during the Investigation phase of the project . Based on the average wind data for

2008 and early 2009, the general direction Of wind In the City of' Huntington Beach Is south-southwest to 
west-southwest with an average velocity of' five <S> kilometer per hour. 
special configurations Of the Site structures with respect to the shape and height aspects Of the structures , 
created an air corridor through which the accelerated wind speed became a maJor factor In carrying 
airborne particles. These particles were deposited in the open area after they had lost their initial speed. 
As demonstrated In Figures (Sheet Wind 3 Of Wind 4 -Wind south-Southwesterly Direction and Sheet Wind 4 Of 
Wind 4 - Wind West-southwesterly Dlrecttonl, a slight change in the direction Of wind from west-southwest 
to south-southwest can change the direction Of channeled wind through the air corridor. The PAHs 
distribution map (Sheet s2 Of s2 - PAHs Distribution Mapl, prepared based on the surface soil samples 
collected during the Investigation phase Of the project, further supports this phenomena. 
Less frequent south-southwest wind was resp0nslble for the small dep0slt area as depicted on the north Of 
the Administration Bulldlng. However, the more frequent west-southwest wind generated the deposit 
detected on the east Of the Police Department building. 
Routine site sweeping and cleaning Of hard surfaces-. and paved areas at the Site has clearly reduced the 
amount Of dust deposits. It should be noted that cleaning of some hard surfaces was performed by the use 
Of vacuum cleaners equipped with HEPA-filters and wet wiping. In the randscaped area, dust from leaves and 
green areas were lifted and collected using HEPA-fitter vacuum cleaners. 
surface soil confirmation sampling was Intended for demonstrating the effectiveness Of cleanup activities at 
the locatlon<sl where dust particles had deposited. 
Two (2) soil samples were collected where elevated concentration Of PAHs had been reported in an area 
north Of the Administration bulldfng. Six (6) soll samples were collected from an area Immediately south 
and southwest Of the council Chamber auditorium. sampling methodology used for soil sampling was 
slmllar to the sampling methodology during the original site investigation. 
The results Of confirmation soil sampling Post-Remediation Indoor wipe sampling tor PAHs are summarlZed in

10.6.2 confirmation of Shallow soil sampling Procedures 
Representative soll surface sampling location selected from a relatively open area. Integrity marked the 
sampling location with gardening flags. No paint 011· other chemicals used to mark the sampling locations. 
Shallow soil samples cs-1 through CS-7 were collected on August 26, 2009 from seven m locations. 
Benzo<alpyrene equivalent sou concentrations of •1.0 mg/ leg had been reported In this area. Army Corps of 
Engineers and DTSC recommended shallow soil sampling protocol for' shallow <upper one-Inch} surface soll 
sampling was followed during the collection of these samples. 
Clean and decontaminated sampling tools consisting of gardening knife and trowel , were placed on plastic 
sheeting. An area of 25-sQuare Inch of undisturbed soll with no vegetation was selected. Using gardening 
knife, the perimeter of a selected area was cut to the recommended depth of one <1l Inch !recommended 
by Army corps of' Engineers}. Using a clean and decontaminated trowel the upper one <11 Inch of the soil 
was collected from the sampling location and placed in unused four <41 ounce glass Jars that had been 
provided by American Testing Laboratory. Following sample retrieval, the glass Jars were sealed with a 
plastic lid, labeled with the sample Identifications , date and time of sampling and project number, and 
Immediately placed into a Zlploc• bag and into a chllled Ice chest using frozen blue Ice. American Testing 
Laboratory provided a courier to transport the samples on August 26, 2009. Chain-of' custody record was 
produced and a request was made to analyze the samples for PAHs content using EPA Method 8310.

rinsed with tap water, and then rinsed with distilled water. All waste effluent generated during this

12.1 conclusions 
12.1.1 Pre-Remediation Period 
Data collected by Integrity during the site characterization period Indicated the presence of environmental 
concerns regarding existing hazardous material at the Site, which warranted proper mitigation and 
remediation . The data collected during this phase can be summarized as followings: 
o Excavation around the foundation for the Site Improvements of the city hall expoSed the Creosote and 
the Metro BUIiders workers suffered skin toxicity from handling and removing the material unprotected 
In June 2008; 
o The Interim Report documented the skin toxicity episode experienced by Metro BUIiders worke rs and

confirmed the presence of high concentrations of PAHs In the Creosote and significant concentrations 
of PAHs In soil, sand, concrete and vermlcullte. It served as the basis for further site characterization; 
o DTSC was Invited and provided guidance for the site characterization and remediation as an outcome of

o Site sampllng Plan was developed and gradually approved by DTSC to fully characterize the site

o Progress Report-1 documented partial the site characterization Including waste stream sampling, soil

Indoor/outdoor wipe sampling for svocs, PAHs and analytical outcomes, and significance of the data;

o Removal Action Workplan was the DTSC-approved legal document for the remediation of the Site and
