site characterization data where Post-Remediation confirmation sampling will be the final step In this 
process. 
12.1.2 During Remediation 
castlerock Environmental initiated the remedial action In late December 2008, and concluded the remedial 
actions for the six <SJ Work Areas on April 9, 2009. All work was performed In accordance with the DTSC 
approved RAW using HazMat-tralned workers with proper personal protection equipment. Health and safety 
meeting were held regularly and preventing measures were enfOrced by site health and safety officer. 
During this period, after securing the work zones, Work Areas 2-6 were excavated. Concrete was cut and 
creosote membrane and associated waste materials were removed. The excavated materials were 
segregated, bagged and placed In secure storage bins. The excavated soil was Placed over polyethy!ene 
sheeting and the stoekpiled materials were covered by Polyethylene sheeting. Profiling analyses were 
performed and the waste materials were categorized and transported to appropriate dispasal facilities with 
fully executed waste manifestation . The excavated areas were properly detailed and cleaned and covered 
with new waterproofing materials for construction related to Seismic Upgrade Project. 
Approximately 247 cubic yards of creosote, and creosote containing or Impacted material and 11 ss-gallon 
drums of contaminated soil and decontamination water were transparted by soc services under proper 
manifestation procedures to Chemical Waste Management • KFH, In Kettleman City, California. 
Integrity staff perfOrmed dally observation and documentation of remediation activities. worker and 
perimeter air sampling, heat stress and noise monitoring and real time measurement of dust emission. The 
collected data was used to maintain health and safety of the workers, as well as onslte and offslte recipient 
by correction of the safety level and maintaining the moist condition during remediation and backfill 
activities around and beyond the fences of Work Areas. 
Integrity Inspected the placement of the vapor barrier and concrete cap at the conclusion of backfilling 
operations. 
12.1.3 post-Remediation sampling 
As a part of the site BMPs, dally cleanup activities were performed around the work areas. The hard surface 
area was cleaned up using sweeping equipment to remove the dust particles generated during excavation 
and backfill actlVltles. The hard surfaces, north and south of the excavation area were cleaned up using a 
HEPA vacuum cleaner. Confirmation air, wipe and soll samples were collected by Integrity to confirm the

12.1.4 General conclustons 
Based on the data presented above, It Is our opinion that: 
o Remedial actions Implemented at the Site were in confOrmance with the DTSC-approved RAW; 
o Mitigated remedies will be effective to reduce the human health risk fOr current and future occupants 
of the property for current or similar site use; 
o contaminated material were properly removed and transported to a treatmenUdlsposal facllltles; 
o Adequate prevention measure were Implied tor health and safety of the workers and field personnel; 
o Adequate prevention measure were Implied tor health and safety Of the Site visitors and pedestrians; 
and 
o overall long-term Impact Of the overall operation on the indoor and outdoor of the Site Is De Minlmls. 
12.2 Recommendations 
Based on the data collected during all phases Of this project and In accordance with the above conctuslons 
Integrity recommends that no further site Investigation or remediation In regard with the defined 
chemicals Of concern and associated with the targeted sources Is warranted. Should additional chemicals or

13.0 WARRANTY 
13.1 Site Assessment 
The field observations, measurements, and research reported herein are considered sUfficlent In detail and 
scope to form a reasonable basis for a site assessment of the Site. The assessment, conduslons, and 
recommendations presented herein are based upon the subJectlve evaluation Of llmlted data. They may not 
represent all conditions at the subject site as they reflect the Information gathered from specific locations. 
The findings described In this report are based on the observations from a select number of areas. Integrity 
warrants that the findings and conclusions contained herein have been promulgated In accordance with 
generally accepted Industrial hygiene principles and methodology and only for the described site. 
13.2 Use by Third Parties 
This report was prepared pursuant to the agreement Integrity with its Client the City. That contractual 
relationship Included exchange Of information about the subject site that was exclusive and between 
Integrity and Its dlent , and it has served as the basis upon which this report was prepared. Because Of the 
exclusive nature Of the communication between Integrity and Its client, reliance or any use of this report by 
anyone other than the client for whom It was prepared, is prohibited and therefore Integrity does not 
assume any responslblllty for Its use by other parties. 
13.3 Unidentifiable conditions 
This site assessment has been developed to provide the dlent with information regarding apparent 
conditions relating to the Site. Although Integrity believes that the findings and conclusions provided In 
this report are reasonable; the assessment Is necessarily limited to the conditions observed and to the 
Information available at the time Of the work. Due to the nature of the work, there is a possibility that 
conditions may exist which could not be Identified within the scope Of the assessment or which were not 
apparent at the time of our site work. The assessment Is also limited to Information available from the City 
at the time it was conducted. It Is also possible that the testing methods employed at the time Of the 
report may later be superseded by other methods. Integrity does not accept responslbillty tor changes In 
the state Of the art. No other warranties are Implied or expressed.

SUBJECT: Review of a Site Contamination Assessment Report for the City of 
Huntington Beach City Hall, Huntington Beach, California. 
PCA: 12018 Site Code: 401457-11 
Arnold Schwarzenegger 
Governor 
The Human and Ecological Risk Division (HERD) reviewed a document titJed ·lnt~rim 
Report, Coal Tar Creosote, Site Contamination, City Hall Seismic Upgrade Project, City of 
Huntington Beach, 2000 Main Street, Huntington Beach, California". The report was 
prepared by Integrity Environmental Consultants Inc., for the City of Huntington Beach. 
Comments on the report are described below.. • 
Background 
At the request of the program; HERD reviewed the site assessment report for the City of 
Huntington Beach City Hail. A site hazard assessment was performed in response to an 
outbreak of dermatitis among construction workers removing coal tar creosote (without 
protective gear) from the building basement on 18th and 191n of June 2008 .. The removal 
process was part of a seismic upgrade of the subject property, where concrete slabs 
and soils were.excavated from basement walls coated with creosote. In the 1970s, the 
coal tar creosote was used as water proofing material and primer to attach sidings on 
the subsurface exterior walls of the build Ing basement and foundation, which was then 
covered befow the ground surface by soils and concrete slabs. 
According to the report, con_struction workers at the site will conduct future excavation 
and cleanup activities (see Appendix D) under the purview of the Occupational Safety 
and Health Administration (OSHA) and South Coast Air Quality Management District 
(SCAQMD). The workers will wear appropriate personal protective equipment and the

will be monitored for volatile organic compounds (VOCs} and polycyclic aromatic 
hydrocarbons (PAHs) .. A benchmark of 100 ppm will be used for volatile organic 
compounds (VOCs) in air. For PAHs in air, a level of 0.1 mg/m3 will be used as a 
. benchmark, based on OHSA permissible exposure limits (PELs) and National Institute 
for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH) Threshold Limit Values (TLVs) for coal tar 
pitch volatiles .. For PAHs in soils, a level of 0.5 mg/kg and 5"0 mg/kg will be used as 
benchmarks for carcinogenic and non-carcinogenic compounds, respectively. For 
surfaces, levels of 150 µg/m2 and 1500 µg/m2, will be us~ as benchmarks for publicly 
accessible and non-accessible areas, respectively. The report is also proposing to use 
the above levels as the remediation goals for the site, if this Is the approach that the City 
of Huntington Beach chooses to take to mitigate exposures to the creosote.· Another 
option that the City is considering is to leave the contamination in._place and cover the 
soils with polyethylene sheeting and the creosote covered walls with waterproofing 
material (page 7). Here, HERD evaluated the proposed sampling, monitoring and 
remediation procedures for protection of the health of future workers and the general 
public at the site. 
Document Reviewed 
HERD reviewed, "Interim Report, Coal Tar Creosote, Site Contamination, City Hall 
Seismic Upgrade Project, City of Huntington Beach,· 2000 Main Street, Huntington 
Beach, California". The report was dated August 7, 2008, and received by HERD on 
August 15, 2008. The report was prepared by Integrity Environmental Consultants Inc., 
for the City of Huntington Beach. 
Scope of Review 
HERD has reviewed this document with emphasis on those aspects that affect the risk to 
human health. HERDs review addressed issues concerning the methodology and 
assumptions of the report. We assume that regional personnel have evaluated the 
sampling of environmental media., Any future changes or addition$ to the document should 
be clearly identified. • 
General Comments 
Overall, HERD found that initial centamfnant assessment at the site was perfonned in . 
an acceptable manner. Bulk material, concrete and soils collected from the Work area 
were analyzed for semi-volatile organic compounds (SVOCs) and.PAHs using USEPA 
methodology 8270C and 8310. As expected, high concentrations of PAHs were 
detected In the various sampled media. The report states that additional soil and 
concrete '!laterial will be collected from around the contaminated area, as well as in

City of Hunti,gton Beach Site Contamination Assessment report 
locations where contamination may have spread during uncontrolled creosote removal 
activities, HERD recommends that these proposed sampling locations be clearly 
identified in figures of the site {see general comments# 1 and 2) and be prese~ted for 
DTSCs review, 
HERD notes that the analytical methodology proposed for sampling and monitoring of · 
voes and svoes at the site may not be appropriate for use in a human health risk 
assessment (comments # 3 and 4 ), due to the fact that the methods may not be 
sensitive or specific enough .. For example, the report is proposing to use a • 
photoionization detector (PIO) to monitor VOCs in air, which does not provide data on 
individual voe compounds. HERD recommends that soils and ambient air in the area 
be sampled for voes using USEPA methodology, so that individual voe compounds 
can be identified and quantitated, for use in a human health risk assessment (see 
comment# 5). 
Additional data collected at the site will be useful in determining appropriate cleanup 
procedures for the site. For example, the City may decide to leave the contamination ln 
place and seal up the basement walls and soils such that exposure pathway to creosote 
exposures in rendered incomplete. In this case, a risk assessment may need to be 
conducted to demonstrate that the voes and SVOCs are not a health concern for 
future worker and the publi_c" If the City decides to remove the contamination using 
procedures outlined in the Health and Safety Plan (Appendix D), a post-remediation risk . 
assessment may need to be conducted to demonstrate that risks/hazards to future 
workers and public are within the risk management range (comment# 7 and 8) .. Toxicity 
values developed by California Environmental Protection Agency (CalEPA) Office of 
Environmental Health Hazard Assessment (OEHHA) and the United S~ates 
Environmental Protection Agency (USE PA) should ,be used in the risk assessment 
(comment# 6). Alternatively, if background PAH concentrations can be established for 
PAHs at the site and the contamination cleaned up to these levels, there may not be a 
need for a risk assess_ment Below are additional comments on the report. 
1) Please provide figures of the site where the buildings are clearly demarcated 
along with all sampling locations. The figures presented in the report are difficult 
to follow due to poor print quaiity and presence of numerous vertical and 
horizontal lines across the pages. • 
2) HERD recommends that the sampling plan (presented in Appendix E) include 
details on sampling locations that will be used to delineate the lateral and vertical 
extent of contamination at the site (specifically in soils and concrete), Figures 
clearly showing the proposed sampling locations should be included in the 
document. The report states that creosote contamination at the site has spread 
(below ground) around the work area, as well as outside and inside the building, 
as a result of uncontrolled coal tar creosote removal, concrete slab busting and

soil excavation from different pof nts on the site in addition to the original 
excavation pit In addition, the pervasive use of the creosote around the exterior 
of the foundation may have contaminated soils, sand, vermiculite and concrete 
slab. 
3) HERD d9es not concur with the use of OSHA or NIOSH regulatory levels as 
background and cleanup levels for PAHs in air at the site. The report is proposing 
to use 100 µg/m3 as the background and cleanup level for PAHs in air, at the site 
(page 14), HERD recommends that concentrations of each of individual PAH 
identified at the site be measured individually using USEPA methodology. (see 
comment# 5), and cleanup levels be established based on hazard/risk and 
background PAH information in soils .. 
4) HERD does not concur with the use of 5 .. 0 mg/kg and 0,5 mg/kg as background 
and cleanup levels for non-carcinogenic arid carcinogenic PAHs, respectively, in 
soils, since these values exceed California Human Health Screening Levels 
{CHHSLs) of 0.13 mg/kg for benzo(a)pyrene and thus may not be protective of 
human health. Background concentrations can be established based on 
concentrations of the compound{s) detected In unaffected ar~as around the 
vicinity of the site, 
5) HERD recommends that voe sampling and analysis in soils and air be 
conducted using guidelines provided in the 2003 Advisory - Active Soil Gas 
gu ldance (http://www.dtsc.ca.gov/LawsRegsPolicies/Policies /S iteCleanup/ 
upload/SMBR ADV activesoilgasinvst.pdf) and DTse•~ 2005 Guidance for the 
Evaluation and Mitigation of Subsurface Vapor Intrusion to lndooi Air 
(http://www.dtsc.ca.gov/AssessingRisk/upload/HERD POL Eval . 
Subsurface Vapor Intrusion interim finaLpdf), voes in soil gas should be 
analyzed using USEPA method 8260B, voes in air should be collected using 
Summa canisters and analyzed using USEPA's T0-15 methodology. The report 
is proposing to monitor total voe concentrations in air using a PIO (page 13). As 
mentioned ea~ier, this methodology does not provide information on the 
concentrations of individual compounds within voe mixtures. For PAH analysis 
in air, HERD recommends using USEPA's method T0-13A Naphthalene and 2-
methylnaphthalene should be analyzed using USEPA's T0-17 methodology, 
since TO-13A has a low collection efficiency for this compound. The 
· recommended US EPA methods have signifi~ntly lower detection limits than the 
OSHA (method 58) and NIOSH (methods 5023 and 55()6), and greater 
specificity, and therefore are more useful for risk assessment purposes., 
6) It is unclear to HER~ how B(a)P equivalents were calculated for the PAHs (see 
section labeled "Samples of June 25, 2008"), HERD recommends following 
guidelines provided in· Office of Envfronmental Health Hazard Assessment's 
(OEHHA) Technical Support Document for Describing Available Cancer Potency

7) HERD recommends that a post remediation risk assessment be conducted to 
ensure that persons at the site are not being exposed to unacceptable

site. Cancer risks and non-cancer hazards from exposure to PAHs (via the 
ingestion, dermal and inhalation pathways) can be estimated for receptors who 
may come in contact with the contamination in· air and soils, using guidelines 
provided in DTSC's Preliminary Endangerment Assessment (PEA) and USEPA's 
Risk Assessment Guidance for Superfund (RAGS). For methodology on

risks/hazards from exposures to compounds associated with creosote, via all 
routes of exposure (ingestion, dermal and inhalation) should meet risk . 
management goals. Alternatively, the site soils and surfaces can be cleaned up 
to background PAH levels, which will need to be established for the site. 
8) HERD recommends that results obtained from surface wipe samples be 
evaluated according to guidance outlined in Appendix D of the 2002 report 
prepared by the Contaminants of Potential Concern (COPC) Committee of the 
World Trade Center Indoor Air Taskforce Working Group and titled 'World Trade 
Center Indoor Air Assessment: Selecting Contaminants of Potential Concern and 
Setting Health•Based Benchmarks" (http://www.tera.org/peer/ WTC/COPC %20-
%20Final%20-%2009-12-02.pdf.). The report is proposing to use a background 
level of 150 ug/m2 for PAHs in "client employees and public accessible areas" 
and a background level of 1500 ugim2 in •client employees and public 
inaccessible areas" (page 14), Please indicate the source of these values. 
9) HERD recommends that a reference for surface wipe sample collection 
methodology be included in the report, along with proposed surface sampling 
locations .. The report states that "the concrete slabs covering the outdoor area 
outside the city hall building is highly likely to be contaminated from the 
construction· and excavation activities and the uncontrolled coal tar creosote 
removal activity'' (page 13).,

HERD recommends that that the guidance provided above be used to delineate the 
nature and extent of creosote associated contamination in soil, air and surfaces at the

From: Ibrahim Sobeih [mailto:im_sobeih@hotmail.com]

Subject: RE: Results of analysis (In Summary and PDF formats) of samples from "Qty Hall Seismic Retrofit 
Project" 
Good Morning Massoud, 
After reviewing the lab analytical results of soil samples collected on 9/4 and 9/5/08, the following are my 
comments: 
Keep in mind the next three points 
First -- The order of sample collection where B51 was collected from Area #4 first, BSS-8S10 collected from the 
soil pile second and BS2A&B-BS4A&B <:0llected last as background samples. 
9/11/2008 
Case 8:26-cv-00348-JWH-ADS     Document 130     Filed 09/09/26     Page 163 of 373   Page
ID #:5140

--- PAGE 164 ---
( 
Page 2 of 3 
Second -- The samples were collected at different depth, BS1 at 3-5 ~, BSS-8S10 as deep as possible In the pile, 
BS2A-BS4A at 6 In, and BS2B-BS4B at 2-3 ft. BS2A and 8S28 were collected from two different but close 
points because the soil at point A was very hard to dig. 
Third -- The samples were analyzed for voes (Method 8260B), SVOCs (Method 8270C) and PAHs (Method 8310). 
All samples revealed non-detectable concentrations of the 67 compounds tested for In this method except 
samples BS3A&B. 
p-isopropyltoloune was detected in BS3A at concentration of 12.4 microgram per kilogram. 
The same compound was detected between the Method Detection Limit and Practical Quantitation Limit for B53B. 
This means that this compound was detected at higher conc.entration in the surface soil (6 in) than at 3-5 ft. 
This compound may have originated from vehicular traffic or other ambient sources and not from the spread of 
sfte oontamination since site tested soil samples from Area #4 revealed non-detectable concentrations of voes. 
PAHs Results 
Pile soil samples - BSS-8S10 revealed varying concentrations above the Method Detection Limit (MDL) of 14 
different PAHs and two PAHs were below the MDL in the 10 samples. 
This is a confirmation of the contamination of the soil pile with coal tar creosote. 
Area #4 sample - 8S1 revealed ooncentrations below the MDL for the 16 PAHs confirming no vertical 
contamination at that sampling point depth. • 
Background soil samples - samples BS4A&B were collected first ~nd revealed concentrations below the MDL for 
the 16 PAHs at 6 inches and 2-3 ft depth. 
Background samples BS3A&B were collected second and sample BS3A revealed concentrations above the MDL for 
6 different PAHs whereas sample 8S3B revealed concentrations below the MDL of 16 PAHs. 
Background samples BS2A&B sere collected third, however, the two samples were collected from two different 
points. 
Background sample BS2A revealed concentrations below the MDL for the 16 PAHs whereas sample 8S28 
revealed concentrations above the MDL for 11 different PAHs higher than any other sample. 
9/11/2008 
Case 8:26-cv-00348-JWH-ADS     Document 130     Filed 09/09/26     Page 164 of 373   Page
ID #:5141

--- PAGE 165 ---
Page 3 of 3 
Cross contamination from the pile samples to the background samples does not appear to be an issue since 
sample BS4A&B were collected after the soil pile sample and it showed PAHs concentrations below their 
respective MDLs. 
Background soil samples contaminations may have originated at the time when the Civic Center complex was 
built or from the use of creosote coated telephone poles or from oil rigs.

Re: 2008-00097 City of Huntington Beach - City Hall SeismicUpgrade Project; Sampling 
Effort of Septemebr 4, 2008 
Below are DTSC's comments re : the sampling_ plan for soil gas and bulk samples . 
Soil Bulk Sampling location Figure: The two additional background samples for soils are too 
close to the contaminated areas. Please pick locations that are at least as far away from the 
contamination as the 1st proposed location. It can be from a near by off-site location (with 
no known PAH or voe contamination, such as a park etc), or the opposite corner of the parking 
lot. Also, please make sure that these background locations are not close to any of those oil 
pumps next to the parking. 
Soil Bulk Sampling location Figure: As far as sampling from the stockpile goes, the six 
samples shouldn't be taking from one point . It should be randomly distributed in the pile 
area, so that there is good coverage. 
Soil Gas Sampling Location Figure: you don't need to be sampling fo r soil gas in the 
background locations. It doesn't serve any purpose at this point. DTSC recommends that you 
stick to the original four proposed locations only. 
Please consider those comments while performing the sampling tomorrow. I will be at the site 
around 8:30 am. 
Thanks

SUBJECT: Review of a Progress Report for the City of Huntington Beach City Hall, 
Huntington Beach, Calffomla. 
PCA: 12018 Site Code: 401457-11 
The Human and Ecological Risk Division (HERD) reviewed a document titled "Project 
Progress Report-1, Coal Tar Creosote, Site Contamination, City Hall Seismic Upgrade 
Project, City of Huntington Beach, 2000 Main Street, Huntington Beach, California• .. The . 
report.was prepared.by Integrity Environmental ·eonsu·1tants Inc" for the City of Huntington 
Beach. Comments on the report are described below. 
Background 
At the request of the program, HERD reviewed the progress report for the City of 
Huntington Beach City Hall. This r:eport presents results of initial soil and soil gas 
sampling events at the site, afong With proposals for additional soil, surface and Indoor 
air sampling. A site contamination assessment (August 7, 2008) was initially performed 
for the City in response to an outbreak of dermatitis among construction workers 
removing coal tar creosote (without protective gear) from the building bas~ment o_n 18th 
and 19th of June 2008, The removal process was part of a seismic upgrade of the 
subject property, where concrete slabs and soils were excavated from basement walls 
coated with creosote. The excavated areas·and materials have since then been covered 
with polyethylene sheeting and waterproofing material has been placed on the creosote 
contaminated walls to minimize further exposures. In the 1970s, the coal tar creosote 
was used as water proofing material and primer to attach sidings on the subsurface 
exterior walls of the building basement and foundation, which was then covered below 
the ground surface by soils and concrete slabs,

City of Huntington Beach Progress report 
According to the report, construction workers at the site will conduct future excavation 
and cleanup activities under the purview of the Occupational Safety and Health 
Administration (OSHA) and South Coast Air Quality Management District (SCAQMD). 
The workers will wear appropriate personal protective equipment and will be monitored 
for volatile organic compounds (VOCs) and polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs). 
The City is proposing to backfill th~ excavated areas with soils that were removed from 
those areas (and stored in stock P.iles at the back of the property), as part of 
remediation measures at the site (communications with City on September 15, 2008). 
Here, HE~D evaluated the proposed sampling, monitoring and remediation procedures 
for protection of the health of Mure workers and the general public at the site. 
Document Reviewed 
HERD reviewed, "Project Progress Report-11 Coal Tar Creosote, Site Contamination, 
City Hall Seismic Upgrade Project, City of Huntington Beach, 2000 Main Street, / 
Huntington Beach, California", The report was dated September 15, 2008, and received 
by HERD on the same·day, The report was prepared by Integrity Environmental 
Consultants Inc.,, for the City of Huntington Beach. 
Scope of Review 
HERD has reviewed this document with emphasis on those aspects that affect the risk to 
human health. HERDs review addressed issues concerning· the methodology and 
assumptions of the rEtport. We assume that regional personnel have evaluated the 
sampling of environmental media, Any future changes or additions to the document should 
be ciear1y identffied. 
General Comments 
In general, HERD concurs with the sampling strategy propc;-,sed in the report for soils, 
surfaces, indoor air and ambient air, The report is propos·ing to collect wipe samples 
from floors and other surfaces of buDdings surrounding the excavation areas 
(Administration Building, Police Building, Council Chambers and Development Building), 
and from concrete surfaces and seating areas in the vicinity of the excavation pits, and 
test these wipe samples for the presence of polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs). 
The report is also proposing to collect volatile organic carbons (VOCs) and PAHs in air 
using TO-13A and TO-15 methods, respectively, from the above-mentioned buildings 
and outdoor areas. These samples will be collected during thr~ rounds of sampling, 
Le", prior to, during and after the excavation pits are backfilled with .soils. HERD 
recommends that the report incorporate modifications recommended by DTSC (in

sampling locations and methodology), during a site walk through, conducted on

Below are HERD's comments regarding the proposed sampling plan. 
1 ) The report should include a section describing remediation measures proposed 
at the site. In a meeting with representatives from the City of Huntington Beach 
and Integrity Environmental Consultants Jnc., (September 15, 2008), the City 
proposed to b~kflll the creosote contaminated pits with the excavated soils, 
along with a concrete cover on top of the ba_ckfilled areas, such that exposures to 
any contaminants in these areas are rendered incomplete. HERD notes that 
reme<;liation at the site may also involve cleaning up contaminated surfaces and 
soils in the vicinity of the excavated areas, if creosote-associated contaminants 
are detected in future sampling events, Please include a section in the report with 
information on these procedures. 
2) The report should include a section with details on how data collected at the site 
will be used to estimate risks to human health. A human health risk assessment 
(HHRA) for potential receptors should be conducted using soil, surface and air 
(ind_oor and ambient) data collected at the site, according to guidelines provided 
in an earlier memo~ndum from DTSC dated August 25, 2008. The risk 
. assessments should be prepared by personnel who are familiar with the 
methodology. 
3) HERD concurs with the conclusions of the report, with regards to voes at the 
site. The report concluded that Voe concentrations were either not detected or 
present in very low concentrations in soils and soil gas at the site. As such, 
risks/hazarqs to the public or employees at the site from a vapor intrusion 
scenario are not a concern, Based on these obsetvatlons, HERO notes that voe 
sampling in indoor air spaces (using T0-15 methodology) may not be necessary. 
HERD, however; has some concerns about the quality of soil gas data collected 
at the site (see specific comment# 6). 
4) HERD recommends that USEPA's TO-17 methodology be used to detect 
naphthalene and 2-methylnaphthalene in indoor and ambient air at the site. The 
report is proposing to use TO~15 methodology ror such purposes. A review of the 
soil QE!S data analyzed usinp TO-15 methodology indicated that the detection limit 
for naphthalene Is 12 ug/m , which is above the indoor air CHHSL for this 
chemical (0.12 ug/m3).. • • • 
5) On September 17, 2008, sampling locations (surface, soils, indoor air and 
ambient air) were identified by Integrity Environmental Consultants Inc., in 
consultation with representatives from DTSC. HERD recommends that 
modifications suggested during this meeting be incorporated into the report.

Specific Comments 
City of Hunlington Beach Progress report 
1) Section 4.4: HERD reoommends that the report include details on procedures for 
soil gas sampling, such as purge volumes, leak check tests, compounds used. 
during the leak check tests, and tubing. Proof that summa canisters (used to 
collect soil gas samples) were certified clean (by the laboratory) should also be 
provided in the report. Details of soil gas sampling procedures to be included In 
this report are provided in DTSC's 2003 soil gas guidance (see memorandum 
from HERD dated August 25, 2008), 
2) Section 4.2.2: Please include a column in the tables (provided in this section), 
where all classes of chemicals (PAHs, voes, SVOCs) that will be analyzed in 
indoor and outdoor air, surfaces and sc;,ils, are dearly identified. 
3) Section 4.2.2: Please note that the purpose of collecting surface, soil and air 
samples before the remediation phase is not to establish background levels, but 
rather to delineate the lateral extent of existing contamination at the site, since 
previous activities at the site may already have resulted in dispersal of 
contaminants throughout the facility. The report states that samples will be 
collected one time before the remediation phase (to establish background levels), 
two times during the remediation phase (to monitor worker exposure) and one 
time after remediation (to monitor city employees' exposure). 
4) Appendix D: Please provide figures of the site where the buildings are clearly 
demarcated along with all proposed sampling locations. Distinct areas within 
each of the buildings should be clearly identified (for example, offices, restrooms, 
kitchen areas, etc). The figures should be prepared according to examples 
provided by OTSC ~t the September 15, ·2ooa, meeting, with representatives 
from th~ City of Huntington Beach and Integrity Environmental Consultants Inc, 
References to all figures should be included in the text, In the current report, 
sampling locations were provided on engineering blueprints with legends that are 
not relevant to this report. 
5) Appendix F: For future reference, please take precautions to ensure that summa 
canisters and cartridges are clean and free of contamination. A review of the soil 
gas data (provided in the report) indicates that several VOCs that are normally 
associated with laboratory contamination (e.g., acetone; tetrahydrofuran etc) and 
cleaning agents (d..fimonene, pinene), were ubiquitously present in all soil gas 
and blank (ambient air) samples. This suggests that the summa canisters may be 
a source of these contaminants. However, HERD notes that a complete history of 
. the site is not known, and as such some of these chemicals may be originating 
from the property itself.

Indoor Air Sampling Scheduled for Wednesday,

Arnold Schwarzenegger 
Governor 
The Department of Toxic Substances Control (DTSC) will oversee the indoor air 
sampling to take place in the City Hall and the Police Department building. SUMA 
'air-monitoring' canisters will be placed at approximately eighteen locations around 
the inside of the building to measure air emissions. These samples are collected 
over a period of a few minutes up to several hours. The samples will be analyzed for 
potentially hazardous chemical concentrations. Also, 'wipe' samples will be taken 
from hard surfaces through-out the building to collect dust for further analysis as 
part of the environmental investigation. The sampling will take place between the 
hours of 7 a.m. and 5 p.m. 
The results of the sampling will be evaluated to determine if further action will be 
required. 
Please do not disturb or remove the canisters. 
If you have any questions regarding these activities, please contact:

Mr. Sayed: 
I am forwarding the waste management/treatment plan for your use. 
As indicated in the Castlerock Environmental e-mail the waste will be characterized prior to the disposal 
at the landfill. 
Based on the previous sampling results by Integrity, the following waste stream is expected to be 
confirmed by any future sampling of the waste materials: 
• Excess soils from Work Areas 1 and 4 - California Class 1 Hazardous Waste; 
• Concrete from the saw cutting of slab in Work Areas 2, 3, 5 and 6 - California Class 1 Hazardous 
Waste; 
• Creosote, sand and vermiculite form Work Areas 2, 3, 5 and 6 - RCRA Hazardous waste; and 
• Waste water from sampling during soil sampling tor the site characterization and any water that 
may be generated during the upcoming remediation effect - California Class 1 Hazardous Waste. 
It is expected that the hazardous material remediation project: backfilling of soils in Work Areas 1 and 
4, saw cutting and cleaning of Work Areas 2, 3, 5 and 6 as well as application of the new waterproofing 
in work areas as may be required per the seismic upgrade project will be completed within four (4) 
weeks. 
We plan to perform the majority of the work during non-business hours or low human traffic period to 
minimize any potential impact on the building occupants and the public. 
A firm schedule is expected to be provided for inclusion in the final RAW, a copy of which will be 
provided to your office as soon as we have received it. 
I will be in the office tomorrow - please contact me if I can be of further assistance.

Dear Mr .. Charlonne: 
This is to notify you that the Department of Toxic Substances Control (DTSC) hereby 
approves the Final Removal Action Workplan (RAW) dated December 18, 2008 for the 
City Hall site, located in Huntington-Seach, California. 
DTSC has determined that the RAW satisfactorily addresses all applicable state and 
federal statutes and regulations 
The 10-day comment period ended on December 13, 2008. DTSC has not received 
any public comments on the draft RAW and the draft Notice of Exemption (NOE). 
If you have any questions, please contact me at (714) 484-5432 or project manager, Mr. 
Safouh Sayed at (714) 484-5478.

INHALATION: Finely divided wood dust, treated or untreated, may cause nose, throat or hmg

possible weakness and incoordination. 
INGESTION: Swallowing treated sawdust may cause mouth, throat and stomach irrllal.ion. Nausea, 
vomiting and diarrhea can occur. 
DELAYED EFFECTS: CREOSOTE PRESERVATIVE: Prolonged and repeated skin exposure over many 
years in the absence of recommended hygiene pracllces may lead to changes in skin 
pigmentation, benign skin growths and may in some cases, result In skin cancer. 
Additionally, inhalation of creosote vapors may presenl a lung r.anccr ha7.ard.

7. HANDLING AND STORAGE 
NORMAL HANDLING: (Always wear recommended personal protective equipment.) 
Wear clothing closed al the neck, long sleeves and non-porous type gloves. Industrial type resistant (e.gq vinyl 
coated) heavy duty Aexible gloves required for prolonged or frequent contact. For dusty operalions (areas), wear 
necessary resistant protective apparel including required head, hand and safety-type footwear. Wear tightly woven 
coveralls or long sleeved shirts and long pants. A complete soap and water shower at the end of each working day is 
recommended for alJ Industrial work situations. 
STORAGE RECOMMENDATIONS: Keep away from open flame.

ventilation In pattern/volume to control inhalation exposures below current exposure limits and areas below 
explosive dust concent.rations.

OTHER DISPOSAL CONSIDERATIONS: 
Treated wood should not be burned in open fires or In stoves, fireplaces or residential boilers because toxic chemicals 
may be produced as part of the smoke and ashes. Treated wood from commercial or industrial use (e.g. construction 
sites} may be burned only In commercial or Industrial incinerators or boilers In accordance with local, state and 
federal regulations. Treated wood is not regulated as a hazardous waste by the USEP A. and usually does not fail the 
US EPA TCLP test. depending on sampling procedures, particle size, type of wood and proporUon of treated to 
untreated wood sample. Dispose of sawdust and wood in accordance with local, state and federal regulations. 
Contact your direct. supplier for additional disposal information. 
Tile information offered here is for the product as shipped. Use and/or alterations to the product such as mixing 
with other materials may significantly change the characteristics of the material and alter the RCRA classlflcatio11 and 
the proper disposal method.

11. TOXICOLOGICAL INFORMATION 
IMMEDIATE (ACUTE) EFFECTS: Possible skin lrrltatlon which is accentuated by sunlight. 
DELAYED (SUBCHRONIC AND CHRONIC) EFFECTS: IARC has classified wood dust as a human carcinogen. 
Norlllal handling of the creosote treated wood would not be expected to generate wood dust, but cutting, grinding 
and/or other activities may generate wood dust. 
OTHER DATA: 
Persons with pre-existing disease or a history of ailments involving the skin or respiratory tract may be at a grealer 
than normal rlsk of developing adverse health effects from woodworking operations with this product. 
The IARC monographs (Vol. 35} states that there is sufficient evidence for the carcinogenicity of cn.•osote in 
experimental animals. The NTP Annual Report on Carcinogens states that creosote oils are carcinogenic in 
experimental animals. Creosote does not appear in the OSHA Subpart Z Table. Epidemiological studies of workers 
in the woodtreatlng industry have shown no significant health effects due to occupational exposure to creosote. 
Many cohort and case-control studies of cancer of the nasal cavities and paranasal sinuses have shown increased risks 
associated with exposure to wood dust. Adenocarcinoma of the nasal cavities and paranasal sinuses is clearly 
associated with exposure to hardwood dust. Occupational exposure to wood dusl does not appear to have a C(lt1sal 
role in cancers of the oropharynx, hypopharynx. lung, lymphatic and haematopoietic systems, stomach. mlon or 
t'. rectum. 
No known Ingredients which occur at greater than 0.1%, olher than those listed above, are listed as a carcinogen .In 
the IARC Monographs on lhe Evaluation of the Carcinogenic Risk of Chemicals to Humans. the NTP Annual Report

Dear Mr. Martin:
The Department of Toxic Substances Control (DTSC) completed our review of the 
revised Soil Management Plan (SMP) dated October 5, 2023, prepared by EEC 
Environmental on behalf of the City of Huntington Beach for the Huntington Beach Civic 
Center Property located at 2000 Main Street, Huntington Beach, California 92648 (Site).
EEC has addressed DTSC’s comments from the submitted draft SMP. DTSC hereby 
approves this SMP. Please notify the DTSC Project Manager, Clayton Larkins, by
e-mail at clayton.larkins@dtsc.ca.gov, at least 10 days prior to conducting activities 
under this SMP, as DTSC may be interested in coordinating a Site visit to observe 
project implementation.

DTSC OVERSIGHT COST ESTIMATE – THE CITY OF HUNTINGTON BEACH CIVIC 
CENTER, LOCATED AT 2000 MAIN STREET, HUNTINGTON BEACH, CALIFORNIA
92648 (SITE CODE: 401457)
Dear Mr. Merid:
Section 25269.5 of the California Health and Safety Code requires the Department of 
Toxic Substances Control (DTSC) to provide a cost estimate for regulatory oversight 
activities at the City of Huntington Beach Civic Center, located at 2000 Main Street, 
Huntington Beach, California 92648 (Site). Enclosed is a cost estimate for the Site 
which includes a schedule of expected activities and estimated staff hours and costs for 
the State of California’s fiscal year beginning July 1, 2023 and ending June 30, 2024. 
The hourly rates used in the cost estimate are DTSC’s 2023-2024 contract estimation 
rates. The schedule and cost estimate will be updated as necessary to reflect changes 
to the project or the rates.
Please note that the cost estimate is only an estimate of projected costs, prepared with 
various assumptions regarding activities and deliverables. DTSC will issue invoices 
based on actual costs. The City of Huntington Beach remains liable for all response 
costs incurred by DTSC pursuant to Health and Safety Code Section 25360 et seq., and 
the Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act 
(CERCLA) 42 U.S.C. section 9601 et seq. DTSC reserves all rights to pursue cost 
recovery under all applicable provisions of state or federal law.
e 
Yana Garcia 
Secretary for 
Environmental Protection 
Department of Toxic Substances Control 
Meredith Williams, Ph.D., Director 
5796 Corporate Avenue 
Cypress, California 90630 
Gavin Newsom 
Governor

Cc: Attorney General’s Office’s- Bureau of Environmental Justice, EJ@doj.ca.gov
Office of Inspector General, U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, OIG.Hotline@epa.gov
 
 
Dear Ms. Rashidfarokhi,
 
I hope this email finds you well. I am writing to follow up on my urgent complaint regarding toxic
hazards at the Huntington Beach Navigation Center (17642 Beach Blvd and 17631 Cameron
Lane), which you acknowledged on 02/17/2025, and informed me on 05/01/2025, you had
referred to your Supervisor at DTSC.
 
Residents of the Huntington Beach Navigation Center, including myself during a 25-month
stay, have faced grave safety risks due to prolonged exposure to toxic contaminants at the site,
operational from December 2020 to present. The facility’s degraded asphalt cap, installed
without maintenance, allowed hexavalent chromium (detected at 980 μg/kg, 3.3× safety limits)
and lead (145 mg/kg, 1.8× limits) to infiltrate soil, groundwater, and air as dust. Over two years
of continuous occupancy, residents inhaled carcinogenic chromium-laden dust, ingested lead-
contaminated soil, and endured chronic health impacts, including respiratory failure, kidney
dysfunction, and neurological harm. Despite documented cases linked to lead-induced
immune suppression and repeated warnings about the cap’s deterioration, regulatory agencies
have dismissed complaints as “outside jurisdiction,” enabling systemic negligence. Vulnerable
populations were knowingly housed on poisoned land, stripped of warnings or protections, in a
clear breach of environmental justice and federal housing safety standards.
 
Given the critical nature of this issue and its potential risks to public health and safety, I kindly
request an update on the status of the review and any actions being taken by your team.
Additionally, please let me know:
 
If there is an estimated timeline for resolving this matter. Whether any further documentation
or information from my side would assist in expediting the process.
I understand the complexity of such cases, but the urgency of addressing these hazards
cannot be overstated. Your prompt attention to this matter is greatly appreciated.
 
Thank you for your time and diligence. I look forward to your response.
 
Best regards,
 
Jesse Knabb

--- PAGE 341 ---
 
I am writing to formally escalate my concerns regarding the ongoing public health crisis at
the Huntington Beach Navigation Center (17642 Beach Blvd, APN 167-472-07 and adjacent
17631 Cameron Lane Property, APN 167-472-08), where toxic soil contamination continues
to endanger unhoused residents, including myself. Despite multiple complaints to
Huntington Beach Code Enforcement and referral by CalEPA (Case #COMP-63266, Referral
#2580974), no corrective action has been taken, and vulnerable individuals remain at risk.  
I am also escalating my concerns regarding the City of Huntington Beach’s systemic failure
to protect public health at the Navigation Center, in stark contrast to its rigorous safeguards
during the City Hall soil cleanup (2008). This discrepancy constitutes environmental racism
and negligence, disproportionately harming unhoused individuals, including myself.
Attached medical records confirm severe health impacts from toxic exposure at the site,
and I am prepared to pursue litigation with the support of the ACLU and Earthjustice if
immediate action is not taken.  
 
1. Critical Issues Requiring Immediate DTSC Intervention
 
- Unremediated Contamination:  
  - Soil tests (EEC Environmental, 2020) confirmed hexavalent chromium (450 μg/kg) and
lead (145 mg/kg) exceeding residential screening levels (DTSC HERO Note 3).  
  - Only 1/3 of contaminated zones were excavated; toxins persist beneath occupied areas
([attached map](link to map)).  
- Health Impacts:  
  - My medical records document recurrent pneumonia, pleural effusion, pneumonitis, kidney
failure, and empyema linked to exposure (attached).  
  - Other residents report similar illnesses, and one individual died of pneumonia after being
expelled from the shelter.  
- Huntington Beach’s Non-Response:  
  - CalEPA referred my complaint to HB Code Enforcement (Referral #2580974) on
02/17/2025. No remediation, testing, or shelter closure has occurred.
 
2. City Hall Soil Cleanup (2008): A Standard of Care 
As detailed in the Removal Action Workplan, the City Hall site received:  
 
- Full excavation of 1,150 cubic yards of PAH-contaminated soil.  
- A concrete cap and “No Further Action” clearance under DTSC oversight.  
- CEQA compliance, air monitoring, and public transparency.  
-Documentation Concealment: Excavation logs, disposal manifests, and lab analyses were
withheld from the public.  
Case 8:26-cv-00348-JWH-ADS     Document 130     Filed 09/09/26     Page 341 of 373   Page
ID #:5318

--- PAGE 342 ---
 
3. Navigation Center: Gross Negligence and Health Impacts:  
 
-The City capped the Navigation Center with asphalt, ignoring subsurface contamination by
hexavalent chromium and lead.
 
 
 
4. Legal Violations: 
 
-“Environmental justice” means the fair treatment of people of all races, cultures, and
incomes with respect to the development, adoption, implementation, and enforcement of
environmental laws, regulations, and policies. (Gov. Code, § 65040.12, subd. (e)).
-CA Health & Safety Code §17974: Failure to abate conditions endangering public health.  
-AQMD Rule 1466: No dust monitoring/suppression, risking PM10 dispersion.  
-CERCLA §103: Failure to report hazardous substance releases.  
 
 
5. Summary of Site Contamination (17642 Beach Blvd, 17631 Cameron Ln) The
Beach Boulevard Property (APN 167-472-07) and adjacent Cameron Lane Property
(APN 167-472-08) in Huntington Beach, CA, were found to have soil contamination
from historical agricultural and residential use. Key contaminants identified:
 
- Lead: Maximum concentration of 145 mg/kg (exceeding the DTSC screening level of 80
mg/kg).
- Hexavalent Chromium: Maximum concentration
of 450 μg/kg, as high as (exceeding the DTSC screening level of 300 μg/kg).
- Additional contaminants on Cameron Lane
Property: Toxaphene (1,600 μg/kg), 4,4-DDE (3,100 μg/kg), and pesticides/herbicides.
 
 
6. OCHCA and DTSC- Recommendations:
 
1. Temporary Use Approval: Contaminated soil
beneath the proposed asphalt cover is deemed safe for temporary homeless shelter use, per
Governor's Executive Order N-32-20.
2. Soil Management:
-DTSC recommended additional tests which were not completed:
"However I would suggest you sample beyond 3 ft bgs and also collect soil gas data."
Case 8:26-cv-00348-JWH-ADS     Document 130     Filed 09/09/26     Page 342 of 373   Page
ID #:5319

--- PAGE 343 ---
This was requested by Robert Senga from DTSC- Robert.Senga@dtsc.ca.gov 
- Excavated soil must be disposed of properly (e.g., Class I landfill) with manifests provided.
- Imported clean fill must be tested and comply with DTSC standards.
3. Future Use:
- Additional remediation required if site use changes (e.g., longer duration than "temporary
use" which would include residents staying 25 months, in addition to permanent housing,).
- Property owner must notify OCHCA of any future changes or new contamination.
4. Infrastructure Precautions:
- Maintain a 2-foot clean soil buffer around utility lines (not completed).
- Obtain permits for well destruction if the irrigation well is encountered.
5. Oversight: Confirmation sampling and adherence to health/safety plans during
construction.
 
7. Demand for Immediate Action:  
 
-To rectify this environmental injustice, I demand the following within 30 days:  
-Immediate closure of the Huntington Beach Navigation Center.  
-Independent retesting of soil/air by DTSC or EPA.  
- Full Remediation: Excavate contaminated soil, replace the asphalt cap with a concrete
barrier, and obtain DTSC’s “No Further Action” clearance.  
- Medical Screenings: Fund testing for all Navigation Center occupants and staff.  
- Public Disclosure: Release all contamination reports and CEQA documents.  
 
8. Legal and Regulatory Escalation: 
 
-If these demands are unmet, I will pursue legal action with Earthjustice and the ACLU of
Southern California to enforce compliance with the California Environmental Quality Act
(CEQA), Title VI of the Civil Rights Act, and state hazardous waste laws. 
-CA Health & Safety Code §17974: Failure to abate conditions endangering public health.  
-AQMD Rule 1466: No dust monitoring/suppression, risking PM10 dispersion.  
-CERCLA §103: Failure to report hazardous substance releases.  
Failure to act will result in litigation under CA Code of Civil Procedure §1085 (writ of
mandate) and federal civil rights complaints. I am also submitting this complaint to:  
 
- U.S. EPA Region 9 (Environmental Justice Division).  
- California Attorney General’s Office (Environment Section).  
- California Department of Public Health.  
- Huntington Beach City Council (demanding an independent audit).

9. Media Transparency: 
 
-This matter will be shared with The Los Angeles Times, OC Register, and local news
affiliates to ensure public accountability.  
 
Attachments**:  
1. Map of incomplete excavation zones containing Cr(VI).  
2. EEC Environmental soil test reports.  
3. Medical records (cytology, metabolic panels, imaging).  
4. Autopsy report of deceased resident.  
5. CalEPA referral #2580974.  
6. Photographic Evidence of Navigation Center Conditions.  
 
The City’s disregard for vulnerable lives is indefensible. I expect a written response within 30
days detailing corrective actions. Failure to act will compel immediate litigation.  
 
Sincerely,  
 
Jesse Knabb
